## (EPS = 0.89, N = 65).

Importance of spring and autumnal weather conditions [SOURCE: Page_99]

According to the BCC calculated over the entire 1970-2013 period, growth indices [SOURCE: Page_99]

were significantly positively correlated to precipitation of October of the previous year [SOURCE: Page_99]

(October n-1) and May of the current year (May n) (Fig. 3.4). For the other months, the BCC [SOURCE: Page_99]

with precipitation were not significant. A significant negative correlation between [SOURCE: Page_99]

temperature and growth index was only found for May n. The BCC for PET were not [SOURCE: Page_99]

96 [SOURCE: Page_99]

Chapitre 3. Relation entre le climat et la croissance radiale des tilleuls argentés Parisiens [SOURCE: Page_100]

significant whatever the considered month. The BCC calculated between total radiation and [SOURCE: Page_100]

growth index were only significantly negative for October n-1. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Significant negative pointer years were identified for 1976, 1998, and 2006 while 1977 [SOURCE: Page_100]

and 1999 were significantly positive. Among growth indices, there were several occurrences [SOURCE: Page_100]

of coupled years, where the first year displayed negative growth and the second year [SOURCE: Page_100]

displayed positive growth. This phenomenon occurred e.g. in 1976/1977 and 1998/1999 (Figs [SOURCE: Page_100]

3.3 and 3.5). The negative years had generally stronger precipitation deficits ( ) that were [SOURCE: Page_100]

close to or higher than -0.5 (Fig. 3.5). For our pointer years, precipitation anomalies     were [SOURCE: Page_100]

higher than temperature anomalies, which stayed close to 0. The negative years were also [SOURCE: Page_100]

characterized by more frequent deficit of precipitation for at least 7 to 12 out of 15 studied [SOURCE: Page_100]

months. Regarding the two significant positive years for growth, anomalies for precipitation [SOURCE: Page_100]

remained either close to 0 or slightly negative, denoting low deficits of precipitation during [SOURCE: Page_100]

five to six months. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Stability of the climate-growth relationship (1970-2013) [SOURCE: Page_100]

The BBC computed during a 30-year moving window between 1970 and 2013 [SOURCE: Page_100]

involved the same meteorological variables at the same monthly scale (Fig. 3.6) than for the [SOURCE: Page_100]

full-period analysis. The BCC were significant for precipitation in October n-1 (most of the [SOURCE: Page_100]

30-y periods) and in May n (for the 1970-1999 period only). Thus, BCC values for [SOURCE: Page_100]

precipitation of October n-1 appeared quite stable with respect to those for May n through the [SOURCE: Page_100]

whole study period. Temperature was not significantly correlated to growth indices. The same [SOURCE: Page_100]

occurred for the relationships between growth indices and PET, and between growth indices [SOURCE: Page_100]

and total radiation, whatever the 30-year temporal window over the 1978-2013 period. [SOURCE: Page_100]

### 3.3.5. Discussion

Radial growth comparison and tree response to climate [SOURCE: Page_101]

To our knowledge, only one prior dendroclimatological study focused on Tilia spp. [SOURCE: Page_101]

conducted by Moir & Leroy (2013) on Tilia x europaea L. and Tilia platyphyllos Scop. Tilia [SOURCE: Page_101]

tomentosa is a fast-growing species with large and well defined rings in the juvenile phase [SOURCE: Page_101]

(Radoglou et al. 2009). In Paris, street trees had a lower growth rate than park trees. Other [SOURCE: Page_101]

common urban hardwood species has been shown to have a reduced growth compare to rural [SOURCE: Page_101]

conspecifics (Quigley 2004; Roberts et al. 2006) but, opposite trends were also observed due [SOURCE: Page_101]

to higher CO$_2$ concentration or less O$_3$ exposures (Gregg et al. 2003; Evans 2010). [SOURCE: Page_101]

Nevertheless, the major difference between trees living in city parks and in streets is the much [SOURCE: Page_101]

larger area of unsealed vegetated ground in parks. It induces more water infiltration and less [SOURCE: Page_101]

short term runoff (Roberts et al. 2006), both increasing soil water storage. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Thus, the limited growth of street trees in Paris could be explained by a lower [SOURCE: Page_101]

photosynthetic carbon assimilation due to water stress (Sieghardt et al. 2005). Water deficit [SOURCE: Page_101]

induces stomata closure and the reduction of carbon uptake through photosynthesis (Petersen [SOURCE: Page_101]

and Eckstein 1988). Furthermore, we have observed that growth rate decreases for street [SOURCE: Page_101]

silver lindens. This could indicate that water stress undergone by trees in streets influenced [SOURCE: Page_101]

directly the amount of aboveground carbon fixed, likely as a trade-off between growth and [SOURCE: Page_101]

storage. Indeed, it has been already demonstrated that trees growing under conditions of high [SOURCE: Page_101]

environmental stress such as severe water stress (Gruber et al. 2012b) show high levels of [SOURCE: Page_101]

stored non-structural carbohydrates (NSCs) associated with, or despite, a reduced growth [SOURCE: Page_101]

(Simard et al. 2013). [SOURCE: Page_101]

98 [SOURCE: Page_101]

Chapitre 3. Relation entre le climat et la croissance radiale des tilleuls argentés Parisiens [SOURCE: Page_102]

The dendrochronological approach could have permitted to study separately the [SOURCE: Page_102]

climate-growth relationships among land-use types and/or age classes. However, the EPS [SOURCE: Page_102]

analysis did not allow separating the respective influence of these factors. To go further, it [SOURCE: Page_102]

would be essential to greatly increase the number of studied trees, especially in parks (Mérian [SOURCE: Page_102]

et al. 2013). At least 53 individuals have been necessary in order to get a 0.86 EPS value [SOURCE: Page_102]

(Table 3.1). However, this number can be reduced for younger trees, as our results showed an [SOURCE: Page_102]

EPS value of 0.83 for only 21 individuals and higher sensitivity to climate. However, a [SOURCE: Page_102]

previous study highlighted the importance of incorporating different aged tree in chronology [SOURCE: Page_102]

in order to have the most representative climatic signal (Wang et al. 2009). [SOURCE: Page_102]

Importance of spring and autumnal weather conditions [SOURCE: Page_102]

Assessing the sensitivity of tree species to climate is essential to better understand [SOURCE: Page_102]

their future behavior under on-going global climate change (Moisselin et al. 2011). In this [SOURCE: Page_102]

study, silver lindens growth was related to precipitation of October n-1 and May n (Fig. 3.4). [SOURCE: Page_102]

The correlation with precipitation was constantly positive, suggesting a strong and positive [SOURCE: Page_102]

influence of a sufficient water supply for growth (Lebourgeois et al. 2003). In addition, the [SOURCE: Page_102]

correlation values for precipitation were also constantly higher than those for temperature, [SOURCE: Page_102]

expressing that precipitation probably play the main role in the stimulation of cambial activity [SOURCE: Page_102]

(Marion, Gričar & Oven 2007). Favorable climate during the autumn n-1 could boost [SOURCE: Page_102]

carbohydrate storage (Michelot et al. 2012), and increase the amount of relative extractable [SOURCE: Page_102]

water, which is important for earlywood formation and bud break during the following spring [SOURCE: Page_102]

(Barbaroux & Bréda 2002). This could explain the positive influence of precipitation in [SOURCE: Page_102]

October n-1. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Interestingly, correlation for precipitation in May n is concomitant with budburst for [SOURCE: Page_102]

Paris urban silver lindens. In diffuse-porous species, the start of radial growth is strongly [SOURCE: Page_102]

99 [SOURCE: Page_102]

Chapitre 3. Relation entre le climat et la croissance radiale des tilleuls argentés Parisiens [SOURCE: Page_103]

linked to buds’ activity and leaf expansion (Essiamah and Eschrich 1986b). Moreover, this is [SOURCE: Page_103]

consistent with the generally accepted idea that the initial growth phase strongly determines [SOURCE: Page_103]

the annual increment (Bréda and Granier 1996; Deslauriers et al. 2007) and silver lindens, as [SOURCE: Page_103]

for beech (Fagus sylvatica L.), could potentially have achieved between 30% to 70% of their [SOURCE: Page_103]

annual increment by the end of June (Lebaube et al. 2000; Lebourgeois et al. 2005). On the [SOURCE: Page_103]

other side, temperatures in May n could reduce cambial activity. In their review, Begum et al. [SOURCE: Page_103]

(2013) pointed out the importance of cumulated elevated temperatures from late winter to [SOURCE: Page_103]

early spring, as affecting the timing of the initiation of cambial reactivation and xylem [SOURCE: Page_103]

differentiation in tree stems. [SOURCE: Page_103]

Negative pointer years coincided with climatic extreme events. These extreme years [SOURCE: Page_103]

are characterized by a high recurrence, during vegetative season, of strongly negative [SOURCE: Page_103]

precipitation anomalies. The most relevant negative year occurred in 1976, recognized at [SOURCE: Page_103]

European level as a severe drought year (Scharnweber et al. 2011; Météo France & Agence [SOURCE: Page_103]

Parisienne du Climat 2015). According to precipitation anomalies during negative years (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_103]

5), it seems that the repetition of water deficit through the current vegetation period influences [SOURCE: Page_103]

the observed low growth rates (Scharnweber et al. 2011). Characteristic droughts in 1976 and [SOURCE: Page_103]

2006 were described as quite similar with strong deficits in precipitation since the autumn n-1 [SOURCE: Page_103]

until summer n (Météo France website). Thus, the drastically reduced growth we observed for [SOURCE: Page_103]

these years is likely explained by the occurrence of strong precipitation deficits in winter n-1 [SOURCE: Page_103]

and spring n, the latter determining earlywood formation and annual increment (Schwinning [SOURCE: Page_103]

et al. 2005a, 2005b). However, as we also observed positive years directly following negative [SOURCE: Page_103]

years (i.e. 1976/1977; 1998/1999), negative years seem to have short-term impact given that [SOURCE: Page_103]

there is a fast recovery of growth after one year (Figs 3.3 and 3.5). This resilience highlights [SOURCE: Page_103]

the efficient use of limited water resources by urban silver lindens that is a desirable trait for [SOURCE: Page_103]

trees in dry environments like urban areas (Jaleel et al. 2008). [SOURCE: Page_103]

100 [SOURCE: Page_103]

Chapitre 3. Relation entre le climat et la croissance radiale des tilleuls argentés Parisiens [SOURCE: Page_104]

The negative correlation between growth and solar radiation in October n-1 could be [SOURCE: Page_104]

explained by sunscalds, often observed by the Paris practitioners (pers. comm.). Switching [SOURCE: Page_104]

from a transient freezing event to solar radiation exposure would lead to a rapid freeze-thaw [SOURCE: Page_104]

cycle leading to significant necrotic areas on the trunk, and predispose damaged trees to [SOURCE: Page_104]

pathogen attacks (Manion 1991). Moreover, it is known that urban trees are more susceptible [SOURCE: Page_104]

to bark temperature fluctuation than forest trees, which may be further increased by reflection [SOURCE: Page_104]

from buildings, snow, and pavement (Tattar 1989). Finally, successive freeze-thaw cycles [SOURCE: Page_104]

during winter have been shown to generate xylem embolism during the thaw step, and thus to [SOURCE: Page_104]

alter next year’s growth (Améglio et al. 2002; Charrier et al. 2015). [SOURCE: Page_104]

Stability of the climate-growth relationships [SOURCE: Page_104]

Taking into account possible changes in tree sensitivity to climate becomes [SOURCE: Page_104]

increasingly important in climate-growth analyses (Wilmking and Myers-Smith 2008). The [SOURCE: Page_104]

moving analysis of climate-growth relationships indicated that the relationships between [SOURCE: Page_104]

precipitation in October n-1 and May n, and growth remained stable through the 1970-2013 [SOURCE: Page_104]

period (Fig. 3.6). However for May n, only the BCC for the 1970-1999 period was significant. [SOURCE: Page_104]

These results would indicate a recent change in this relation with May n precipitation while [SOURCE: Page_104]

the relation with precipitation in October n-1 remained stable (Andreu et al. 2007; Olivar et [SOURCE: Page_104]

al. 2015). [SOURCE: Page_104]

Remarkably, the significant relationships with temperature and solar radiations found [SOURCE: Page_104]

in the global analysis for the 1970-2013 period appeared not significant in moving analysis, [SOURCE: Page_104]

meaning that temperature-growth and solar radiation-growth relations were not stable through [SOURCE: Page_104]

this period. This could be due to a quick change in temperature evolution in Paris, as a general [SOURCE: Page_104]

increase of 0.3°C per decade of both minimal and maximal temperatures has been observed [SOURCE: Page_104]

since the second part of the 20th century (Météo France & Agence Parisienne du Climat [SOURCE: Page_104]

101 [SOURCE: Page_104]

Chapitre 3. Relation entre le climat et la croissance radiale des tilleuls argentés Parisiens [SOURCE: Page_105]

2015). These results agreed with several dendrochronological researches that demonstrated a [SOURCE: Page_105]

change in the response of tree growth to temperature over the last 40 to 50 years, possibly [SOURCE: Page_105]

attributed to climate changes (Biondi 2011). [SOURCE: Page_105]

### 3.3.6. Conclusions

Water availability clearly appeared to be the main limiting factor of past growth of [SOURCE: Page_105]

street trees. Younger urban silver lindens presented a high sensitivity to climate and the [SOURCE: Page_105]

highest growth rate when compared to older classes. When compared with park trees, street [SOURCE: Page_105]

trees had higher sensitivity but lower growth rates. Climatic and pointer years analyses [SOURCE: Page_105]

pointed out the importance of drought characterization in order to understand its potential [SOURCE: Page_105]

impact on tree functioning. In addition, precipitation occurring in autumn and spring mainly [SOURCE: Page_105]

determined the amount of annual increment. Thus, a better irrigation management, especially [SOURCE: Page_105]

for 15 year-old trees and specifically in October and May, could optimize street tree [SOURCE: Page_105]

functioning and survival. Finally, a stronger stability between growth and precipitation was [SOURCE: Page_105]

only evidenced in October. For the other climatic factors, this study revealed an unstable [SOURCE: Page_105]

relation and so, quick changes in growth-climate relationships over the last 40 years, [SOURCE: Page_105]

underlying the increasing effect of climate change on urban ecosystem sustainability. Under [SOURCE: Page_105]

climate change context, more long-term studies on street tree functioning are needed in order [SOURCE: Page_105]

to follow and predict their future needs and behaviors in urban areas for adapted management [SOURCE: Page_105]

practices. [SOURCE: Page_105]

## DYNAMIQUE       SAISONNIERE   DES RESERVES        GLUCIDIQUES    ET LIPIDIQUES

156 [SOURCE: Page_159]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_161]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]
- Parent_Topic: 1529765
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 4.1. présentation
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
### 4.1. Présentation

Ce second chapitre a pour but d’étudier la dynamique intra-saisonnière des réserves carbonées des arbres plantés en rue comparés à ceux plantés en parc et en arboretum selon 3 classes de DBH. Ces résultats sont analysés face à des mesures de croissances radiales sur le long terme afin de comparer l’allocation du C des tilleuls argentés selon leur milieu de vie et leur âge. Cette étude a été réalisée en collaboration avec Claire Damesin (PR) et Chantal Fresneau (CR) du Laboratoire d’Écologie, Systématique et Évolution (UMR 8079 ESE) de l’Université Paris-Sud. [SOURCE: Page_161]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]
- Parent_Topic: 1529765
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 4.2. principaux résultats
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
### 4.2. Principaux résultats

Les résultats sur les jeunes tilleuls argentés urbains ont montré une croissance radiale plus élevée associée à l'accumulation du saccharose et de d'amidon. Les arbres les plus âgés quant à eux accumulent également des réserves sous la forme de lipides. Ainsi, il semble que les plus jeunes arbres favoriseraient l’allocation du C dans la croissance plutôt que dans la mise en réserve, à l’inverse des arbres les plus âgés. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Cette étude a également souligné que les plus vieux arbres plantés en rue ont une croissance radiale considérablement réduite associée à un stockage plus élevé de lipides et d'amidon par rapport aux arbres en arboretum. Les arbres en milieu urbain pourraient alors prioriser le stockage du C selon une stratégie de survie à long terme et ce, au détriment de la croissance radiale. Cette allocation préférentielle du C dans les réserves pourrait exposer les arbres urbains à des déficits en C temporaires, susceptibles de rendre ces arbres vulnérables face à un environnement de plus en plus contraignant. [SOURCE: Page_161]

## SILVER LINDENS IN PARIS STREETS

A. A. J. David^1, C. Fresneau^3, J. C. Lata^1,2, C. Damesin^3, S. Barot^1, Y. Kraepiel^1, L. Abbadie^1, J. Ngao^4 [SOURCE: Page_162]

1 Sorbonne Universités, UPMC Univ. Paris 06, IRD, CNRS, INRA, UPEC, Univ Paris Diderot, Institute of Ecology and Environmental Sciences, iEESParis, 4 place Jussieu, 75005 Paris, France [SOURCE: Page_162]

---
### 4.3.1. Abstract

Urban tree health and survival are serious issues for practitioners and understanding [SOURCE: Page_163]

street tree functioning under urban chronic water stress is becoming essential. Moreover, [SOURCE: Page_163]

highlighting differences between different tree ages are also important in order to expect a [SOURCE: Page_163]

better management practice. In this respect, tree growth and reserve storage could be used as [SOURCE: Page_163]

good indicators for understanding carbon allocation under water stress. The present study was [SOURCE: Page_163]

conducted on street silver lindens in Paris city according to different DBH classes used as a [SOURCE: Page_163]

proxy of tree age and compared with trees living in parks and arboretum. The concentration of [SOURCE: Page_163]

both lipids and carbohydrates stored for 2015 were assessed in the light of long-term radial [SOURCE: Page_163]

growth study. Results on young urban silver lindens have shown higher radial growth [SOURCE: Page_163]

associated with sucrose and starch accumulation, when older trees of about 50 year-old could [SOURCE: Page_163]

also store reserves in lipids form. The absence of lipids reserves for young trees could indicate [SOURCE: Page_163]

that long-term survival strategy only occur in silver lindens when radial and root growth is [SOURCE: Page_163]

totally achieved. This analysis also pointed that the oldest street trees (over 45 year-old) have [SOURCE: Page_163]

a drastically reduced radial growth combined with higher storage of lipids and starch when [SOURCE: Page_163]

compared to arboretum trees. It should suggest that silver lindens could regulate their C [SOURCE: Page_163]

reserves in a long-term survival strategy at the expense of short-term radial growth. However, [SOURCE: Page_163]

under chronic water stress in streets, this preferential allocation of C in reserve storages could [SOURCE: Page_163]

expose them to regular temporary C deficit. Under climate change context and the increasing [SOURCE: Page_163]

frequency and intensity of drought events as well as precipitations variability and [SOURCE: Page_163]

temperature, these results could suggest a higher risk of drought-induced mortality for street [SOURCE: Page_163]

silver lindens. [SOURCE: Page_163]

Key words: [SOURCE: Page_163]

Urban tree; Carbohydrate reserves ; Lipid reserves ; Radial growth ; Silver lindens [SOURCE: Page_163]

### 4.3.2. Introduction

Urban trees have been increasingly studied in the last decades because of their crucial [SOURCE: Page_164]

role in urban environment. Understanding urban trees functioning is a key issue for urban [SOURCE: Page_164]

planning and the assessment of ecosystem services such as tree cooling effect, air quality and [SOURCE: Page_164]

CO$_2$ storage (Pataki et al. 2011; Díaz-Porras et al. 2014). Nevertheless, several previous [SOURCE: Page_164]

studies pointed that trees in urban environment have a reduced growth and life expectancy [SOURCE: Page_164]

associated with a higher annual mortality rate (Sæbø et al. 2003; Roman and Scatena 2011) [SOURCE: Page_164]

when compared to trees living in more natural environments such as arboretums or forests. [SOURCE: Page_164]

This is especially true for newly planted young trees (Lu et al. 2010). Focusing on tree [SOURCE: Page_164]

survival in Paris (France), city foresters estimate a life expectancy of 80 years for street trees [SOURCE: Page_164]

and a mean mortality rate of around 6% that can be much higher for younger trees (Mairie de [SOURCE: Page_164]

Paris 2016; pers. Comm. at Paris Green Space and Environmental Division). [SOURCE: Page_164]

Such high mortality rates can be explained by the impact of urban environment. [SOURCE: Page_164]

Indeed, photosynthesis and evapotranspiration are likely affected by urban constraints such as [SOURCE: Page_164]

injuries to trunks and branches, intensive pruning, pollution, luminosity interference from [SOURCE: Page_164]

surrounding buildings or water stress. However, as many recent tree mortality events in non- [SOURCE: Page_164]

urban forests have been linked to drought (Allen et al. 2010), numerous studies on street trees [SOURCE: Page_164]

indicate that water stress could be the main factor explaining street tree mortality (Roberts [SOURCE: Page_164]

1977; Wilson 1977; Foster 1978; Foster and Blaine 1978; Steiner 1980; Tattar 1980; Staby [SOURCE: Page_164]

1981; Apple and Manion 1986; Whitlow and Bassuk 1987; Clark and Kjelgren 1989; [SOURCE: Page_164]

Whitlow et al. 1992; Sæbø et al. 2003; Sieghardt et al. 2005; McCarthy and Pataki 2010; [SOURCE: Page_164]

Gillner et al. 2013; Vico et al. 2014). In streets, where the ground surface is highly impervious [SOURCE: Page_164]

and trees are not irrigated, the reduction of precipitation infiltration into soils may increase [SOURCE: Page_164]

water stress (Clark and Kjelgren 1989; Whitlow et al. 1992). It is also generally predicted that [SOURCE: Page_164]

161 [SOURCE: Page_164]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_165]

trees in urban sites have higher water losses than trees in natural forests due to increased [SOURCE: Page_165]

temperature and thus to high evapotranspiration demands (McCarthy and Pataki 2010). In [SOURCE: Page_165]

addition, the accumulation of drought events during tree life could be an important driver [SOURCE: Page_165]

behind the lower growth and life expectancy of street trees (Ciais et al. 2005; Gillner et al. [SOURCE: Page_165]

2013). There is little knowledge about street tree responses to chronic water stress, which [SOURCE: Page_165]

raises difficulties to predict their future behaviors in a changing climate. In the climate change [SOURCE: Page_165]

context, drought may become more common in the future (Moisselin et al. 2011). Higher [SOURCE: Page_165]

temperatures will also increase evapotranspiration demands and a higher intra-annual [SOURCE: Page_165]

variability in precipitation could disturb plant water balance. Thus, a better understanding of [SOURCE: Page_165]

street tree responses to drought can provide insights on their answer to future climatic [SOURCE: Page_165]

conditions and potentially help to prevent events of high tree mortality in urban areas. [SOURCE: Page_165]

McDowell et al. proposed in 2008 that both hydraulic failure and carbon (C) starvation [SOURCE: Page_165]

occurs in trees during drought and can increase their mortality. Although these mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_165]

are not exclusives and could both appear, tree responses to water stress depend on the ability [SOURCE: Page_165]

of a species to avoid or tolerate a very low water potential (Choat et al. 2012; Manzoni et al. [SOURCE: Page_165]

2013; Klein 2014; Martínez-Vilalta et al. 2014; Roman et al. 2015). The water potential of an [SOURCE: Page_165]

individual tree will determine a variety of physiological processes (Hsiao 1973; Ditmarová et [SOURCE: Page_165]

al. 2010). According to drought severity, different physiological responses could arise such as [SOURCE: Page_165]

leaf shedding, reduced transpiration and photosynthesis, reduced growth and adjustment in [SOURCE: Page_165]

the partitioning of assimilates to roots and storage (McDowell et al. 2008). [SOURCE: Page_165]

An abundant literature highlights that drought-induced mortality is strongly related to [SOURCE: Page_165]

a carbon reserve depletion (Piper 2011; Galiano et al. 2012; Adams et al. 2013; Mitchell et al. [SOURCE: Page_165]

2013b; Galvez et al. 2013; Hartmann et al. 2013). A part of the newly acquired C is diverted [SOURCE: Page_165]

into storage through non-structural carbohydrates (NSC) such as soluble sugars and starch, or [SOURCE: Page_165]

162 [SOURCE: Page_165]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_166]

lipids in “fat trees” such as lindens, and which do not directly promote growth. This storage of [SOURCE: Page_166]

resources enables plants to start growth early in spring or/and to survive in unfavorable years. [SOURCE: Page_166]

For instance, during a drought, the regulation of evapotranspiration through stomata closure is [SOURCE: Page_166]

accompanied by a decrease of photosynthetic assimilation of C (Weber and Gates 1990). In [SOURCE: Page_166]

the short term, a temperature increase associated with drought could lead to a negative C [SOURCE: Page_166]

balance (Zhao et al. 2013) due to metabolic demands (i.e.: maintenance and respiration, Bréda [SOURCE: Page_166]

et al. 2006) leading tree to draw on stored reserves. Reserve accumulations could also [SOURCE: Page_166]

increase the osmotic pressure of cells, which prevents the dehydration of tissues. Thus, stored [SOURCE: Page_166]

NSC could be determinant for drought resistance of trees (McDowell et al. 2011; Sala et al. [SOURCE: Page_166]

2012). [SOURCE: Page_166]

However, results are still controversial and, in some cases, no relation between [SOURCE: Page_166]

drought-induced mortality and C depletion (Anderegg et al. 2012; Gruber et al. 2012a; [SOURCE: Page_166]

Mitchell et al. 2013b) and no change in C reserve dynamics have been found (Sala and Hoch [SOURCE: Page_166]

2009; Galvez et al. 2011; Woodruff and Meinzer 2011; Anderegg 2012; Anderegg et al. [SOURCE: Page_166]

2012). It has been recently suggested that trees under water stress could actively regulate NSC [SOURCE: Page_166]

pool as a sink directly in competition with growth (Sala et al. 2012). This strategy could [SOURCE: Page_166]

ensure both long-term survival and short-term growth when necessary, as it is generally [SOURCE: Page_166]

admitted that growth is considered to be more sensitive to drought than assimilation (Sala et [SOURCE: Page_166]

al. 2010). So, the often observed drought-related reduced growth could be more due to the [SOURCE: Page_166]

constitution of C reserve than to the lack of assimilated C per se (Körner 2006; Millard et al. [SOURCE: Page_166]

2007; Sala and Hoch 2009; Woodruff and Meinzer 2011). Indeed, trees growing under [SOURCE: Page_166]

conditions of high environmental stress such as severe water stress (Bréda et al. 2006; Gruber [SOURCE: Page_166]

et al. 2012a) or defoliation (Hoch 2005; Palacio et al. 2008) showed high levels of stored [SOURCE: Page_166]

NSCs despite a reduced growth (Hoch et al. 2003; Millard et al. 2007; Gruber et al. 2012a; [SOURCE: Page_166]

Simard et al. 2013). [SOURCE: Page_166]

163 [SOURCE: Page_166]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_167]

This seemingly contradictory results show that the links between NSC pool, growth and the [SOURCE: Page_167]

severity of water stress remain unclear. Moreover, C allocation has been little studied in street [SOURCE: Page_167]

tree (Weber and Gates 1990). C allocation, through radial growth and C storage, could be [SOURCE: Page_167]

different for tree facing water stress in streets compared those living in less constraining [SOURCE: Page_167]

environments such as park or arboretum. In addition, high mortality rates in city involves [SOURCE: Page_167]

numerous new plantations each year, which leads to a very wide age range in street trees [SOURCE: Page_167]

when compared to non-urban forests. As tree physiology changes with time, street trees of [SOURCE: Page_167]

different age could present different patterns of C allocation (Clark and Matheny 1992; Genet [SOURCE: Page_167]

et al. 2010). [SOURCE: Page_167]

Silver linden represents one of the four predominant species planted in urban areas in [SOURCE: Page_167]

Paris. A previous work estimated the long-term growth of this species through [SOURCE: Page_167]

dendrochronological measurements on tree-ring widths on the 1970-2013 period (David et al. [SOURCE: Page_167]

2016). The aim of this work is to characterize the impact of the plantation location (street or [SOURCE: Page_167]

park/arboretum) on both the seasonal dynamics of reserves and the radial growth of silver [SOURCE: Page_167]

linden trees. During the year 2015, minimal and maximal carbohydrates and lipids [SOURCE: Page_167]

concentrations were assessed one week after budburst and in winter after leaves fall, [SOURCE: Page_167]

respectively, on young and adult silver lindens growing in contrasted urban and peri-urban [SOURCE: Page_167]

environments (namely: streets, park and arboretum). The impact of these environments was [SOURCE: Page_167]

investigated under two main hypotheses: (i) the location might impact tree growth and reserve [SOURCE: Page_167]

storage and (ii) this impact might be age-dependant. [SOURCE: Page_167]

164 [SOURCE: Page_167]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_168]

### 4.3.3. Material and methods

Sites description [SOURCE: Page_168]

This study was conducted in Paris, France (48,8534N; 2,3488E). Parisian climate is [SOURCE: Page_168]

temperate, sub-Atlantic (Crippa et al. 2013). During the studied year 2015, the annual mean [SOURCE: Page_168]

air temperature was of 13.41°C ; it reached for the coldest month 4.9°C in February, while for [SOURCE: Page_168]

the warmer month in July it reached 22°C (Montsouris meteorological station (Paris) [SOURCE: Page_168]

48.8566N; 2.3366E, Météo France station 07156). Sum of annual precipitations in 2015 [SOURCE: Page_168]

reached 506.5 mm, with a monthly maximum in August (80.3 mm) and minimum in [SOURCE: Page_168]

December (21.6 mm). It is worth noting that monthly precipitations in July reached rather low [SOURCE: Page_168]

51.9 mm. [SOURCE: Page_168]

Experimental design [SOURCE: Page_168]

The study focused on silver linden (Tilia tomentosa Moench) which is a predominant [SOURCE: Page_168]

species planted in the streets of Paris - after Platanus acerifolia (Aiton) Willd., Aesculus [SOURCE: Page_168]

hippocastanum L. and Styphnolobium japonicum (L.) Schott - and represents more than 10 % [SOURCE: Page_168]

of all planted trees in Paris streets (Apur 2010b). Silver linden is a diffuse-porous specie [SOURCE: Page_168]

(Schweingruber 1990b) which is known to tolerate quite well urban conditions (pruning, heat [SOURCE: Page_168]

and pollution) and is not subjected to severe phytopathologies (Daniels 1975; Mitchell and [SOURCE: Page_168]

Jobling 1984; Dirr 1990; Gilman 1997; Bassuk et al. 2009; Fini et al. 2009; Sjöman and [SOURCE: Page_168]

Oprea 2010; Ţenche-Constantinescu et al. 2015). Silver lindens are also shade-tolerant [SOURCE: Page_168]

(Radoglou et al. 2009; Ţenche-Constantinescu et al. 2015), an important characteristic for [SOURCE: Page_168]

street plantations. Concerning C storage in the genus Tilia, in addition to carbohydrates [SOURCE: Page_168]

storage, previous studies found a high concentration of lipids in the aerial parts that could [SOURCE: Page_168]

exceed starch concentrations (Höll and Priebe 1985). [SOURCE: Page_168]

165 [SOURCE: Page_168]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_169]

In order to assess the impact of urban environment on growth and storage, different [SOURCE: Page_169]

trees were chosen in three different growing environments (hereinafter referred as locations): [SOURCE: Page_169]

15 within Paris streets, 15 in Paris parks and 5 in the National Arboretum of Chèvreloup (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_169]

4.1). The two first locations are considered in the study as urban environment, the third [SOURCE: Page_169]

location as a non-urban one. All trees were selected according to three classes of DBH [SOURCE: Page_169]

(Diameter at Breast Height, 1.30 m). DBH was preliminary used as a proxy of tree age [SOURCE: Page_169]

because no information, such as plantation date, was available. Class 1 included trees with [SOURCE: Page_169]

DBH between 6 to 15 cm (young trees), Class 2 with DBH between 32 to 43 cm (young [SOURCE: Page_169]

adults), and Class 3 with DBH between 56 to 74 cm (adults) in street and park sites. In the [SOURCE: Page_169]

arboretum site, only the Class 3 was present. The health status of each tree was visually [SOURCE: Page_169]

assessed according to a visual tree assessment (VTA) protocol (Mattheck and Breloer 1994) [SOURCE: Page_169]

and only vigorous trees, without severe wounds or disease, were selected. The age of each [SOURCE: Page_169]

tree and ring-width was determined by using wood core samples and dendrochronological [SOURCE: Page_169]

methods fully explained in a previous study (David et al. 2016). Total tree height and crown [SOURCE: Page_169]

height were measured with an electronic clinometer (Haglöf EC II). The DBH and canopy [SOURCE: Page_169]

spread in four direction were measured using a measuring tape. Crown volume was estimated [SOURCE: Page_169]

using the ellipsoid formula for volume. [SOURCE: Page_169]

For assessing intra-annual dynamic in lipid and carbohydrate storages, tree cores were [SOURCE: Page_169]

collected twice in 2015. These collecting dates were chosen at two important phenological [SOURCE: Page_169]

stages: one week after budburst in the week of the 20th April, when storages were minimal, [SOURCE: Page_169]

and when all leaves just felt and storages process should be maximal in the week of the 23th [SOURCE: Page_169]

November. At each sampling event, one core of 7 to 9 cm long was collected on each tree [SOURCE: Page_169]

with a sterilized Pressler increment borer (coretax HAGLOF; length: 400 mm; diameter: 5 [SOURCE: Page_169]

mm) in order to retrieve the five last tree-rings. Due to Paris city prevention policy for trees’ [SOURCE: Page_169]

health, only one core was sampled on each tree at each sampling event. [SOURCE: Page_169]

166 [SOURCE: Page_169]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_170]

Non-structural carbohydrates extraction and determination [SOURCE: Page_170]

Soluble sugars (glucose, fructose, sucrose) and starch were extracted from 10 mg (± [SOURCE: Page_170]

0.5 mg) of finely ground dry matter from the last 5 years rings pooled together. Samples were [SOURCE: Page_170]

ground using a ball mill (MM 301, Retsch, Germany) with 5 mm balls shaken at 30 Hz three [SOURCE: Page_170]

times for 2 min. Soluble compounds were extracted twice with 1 mL of boiling 80% ethanol. [SOURCE: Page_170]

These 2 ml were pooled and centrifuged. The supernatants and the pellets containing starch [SOURCE: Page_170]

were dried in a centrifugal vacuum concentrator to remove ethanol (Centrivap, Labconco, [SOURCE: Page_170]

USA). Soluble sugars and the pellets containing starch were then rehydrated with 0.02 mol L-1 [SOURCE: Page_170]

NaOH. Concentration of glucose, fructose and sucrose were measured enzymatically by the [SOURCE: Page_170]

method described in and Brummer and Cui (2005). Their concentrations were then added to [SOURCE: Page_170]

determine the concentration of total soluble sugars. Starch was quantified using the same [SOURCE: Page_170]

enzymatic method described for soluble sugars. The concentrations of total non-structural [SOURCE: Page_170]

carbohydrates (TNSC), starch and total soluble sugars were expressed in grams of reserve [SOURCE: Page_170]

compound per 100 grams of dry weight (g 100 g DW-1). [SOURCE: Page_170]

Lipids extraction and determination [SOURCE: Page_170]

Lipids were extracted from 50 mg (± 0.5 mg) of finely ground dry matter from the last [SOURCE: Page_170]

5 years rings pooled together. Samples were ground using a ball mill (MM 301, Retsch, [SOURCE: Page_170]

Germany) with 5 mm balls shaken at 30 Hz three times for 2 min. Lipids were extracted with [SOURCE: Page_170]

2 mL of methanol. These 2 mL were then boiled in a water-bath at 70°C during 3 minutes. [SOURCE: Page_170]

Then 2 mL of chloroform were added and the samples were cooled during 15 minutes in ice. [SOURCE: Page_170]

After this, 2 mL of distillated water were added to the solution and samples were centrifuged [SOURCE: Page_170]

at 2000g and 15°C during 10 min. Chloroform phase containing lipids was then aspirated [SOURCE: Page_170]

carefully (e.g., with a Pasteur pipette) and placed in a weighted glass tube. Chloroform was [SOURCE: Page_170]

evaporated using air flux and the glass tube was weighted a second time. Lipids were [SOURCE: Page_170]

167 [SOURCE: Page_170]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_171]

quantified as the difference between the first and the second weighing and expressed in grams [SOURCE: Page_171]

of reserve compound per 100 grams of dry weight (g 100 g DW-1). [SOURCE: Page_171]

Statistical Analysis [SOURCE: Page_171]

Statistical analyses were performed using R 3.2.0 (R Development Core Team [SOURCE: Page_171]

2011). The effects of both tree growing environment and DBH classes were then [SOURCE: Page_171]

tested. Dendrometric variables (DBH, height, crown volume) were analyzed with an [SOURCE: Page_171]

ANOVA. Age differences between groups were performed using a non-parametric Kruskal- [SOURCE: Page_171]

Wallis test followed by a pairwise Wilcoxon-Whitney test. Ring width differences between [SOURCE: Page_171]

DBH class and growing environments were tested with a linear mixed-effect model with [SOURCE: Page_171]

« year » and « tree » as random effects. TNSC and lipids concentration differences between [SOURCE: Page_171]

April and November 2015 were then tested according a general mixed linear model using [SOURCE: Page_171]

« tree » as a random effect. The difference between November (winter) and April (budburst), [SOURCE: Page_171]

expressing reserves stored in 2015 and hereafter referred as storage, was also calculated for [SOURCE: Page_171]

each group and each compound. Storages were then tested using ANOVA. These models were [SOURCE: Page_171]

then subjected to multiple comparisons of means using either Tukey post-hoc for ANOVA [SOURCE: Page_171]

analyses or Tukey contrasts post-hoc for mixed-effect models. For all statistical tests, the null [SOURCE: Page_171]

hypothesis was rejected for p < 0.05 and significance was represented as follows: “***” when [SOURCE: Page_171]

p ≤ 0.001, “**” for 0.001 < p ≤ 0.01, “*” when 0.01 < p ≤ 0.05, and “.” when 0.05 < p ≤ 0.1. [SOURCE: Page_171]

168 [SOURCE: Page_171]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_172]

### 4.3.4. Results

Tree sample description [SOURCE: Page_172]

Silver lindens groups were made according to DBH and age. Trees in Class 3 had [SOURCE: Page_172]

higher DBH (Arboretum: 71.90 ± 11.51 cm; Street: 64.90 ± 2.37 cm; Park: 60. 6 ± 2.50 cm) [SOURCE: Page_172]

when Class 2 had intermediate values (Park: 41.00 ± 2.50 cm and Street: 39.67 ± 1.23 cm) [SOURCE: Page_172]

and Class 1 lower values (Park: 12.84 ± 1.88 cm and Street: 14.32 ± 1.23 cm; Table 4.1). The [SOURCE: Page_172]

total height of trees did no vary as DBH did, as no significant difference was observed [SOURCE: Page_172]

between smaller trees of Class 1 in parks and Class 1 and Class 2 in streets (averaging 6.42 ± [SOURCE: Page_172]

0 .71 m, 8.42 ± 0.54 m and 14.04 ± 1.36 m, respectively; Table 4.1). Medium heights were [SOURCE: Page_172]

found in trees from Class 2 and 3 in street and park (Class 2 – Street: 14.04 ± 1.36 m; Class 2 [SOURCE: Page_172]

– Park: 15.10 ± 2.32 m; Class 3 – Street: 18. 62 ± 1.95 m and Class 3 – Park: 19.42 ± 1.60) [SOURCE: Page_172]

when trees living in arboretum were the tallest (24.34 ± 2.99 m). As expected, lower crown [SOURCE: Page_172]

volumes were observed in Class 1 (Street: 0.24 ± 0.02 m^3 and Park: 0.19 ± 0.04 m^3; Table [SOURCE: Page_172]

4.1) when the biggest ones were in Class 3 (Street: 1.25 ± 0.20 m^3; Park: 2.32 ± 0.53 m^3 and [SOURCE: Page_172]

Arboretum: 2.72 ± 0.60 m^3). [SOURCE: Page_172]

Tree age and annual growth [SOURCE: Page_172]

The oldest trees were observed for Class 3 in streets (82 ± 4 y; Table 4.1), and had the [SOURCE: Page_172]

same age as trees from Class 3 in arboretum (68 ± 12 y). Trees from Class 2 in streets and [SOURCE: Page_172]

Class 2 and 3 in parks had intermediate ages with respectively 51 ± 2; 40 ± 8 and 48 ± 7 y. [SOURCE: Page_172]

Younger trees were from Class 1 with 13 ± 1 and 12 ± 2 y in street and park respectively. [SOURCE: Page_172]

Interestingly, trees from Class 3 in street had the lowest growth with mean ring width of [SOURCE: Page_172]

2.17± 0.40 mm (Table 4.1) on average while trees in the arboretum with an equivalent age [SOURCE: Page_172]

had the highest growth (5.97 ± 0.84 mm). Trees around 50 years old in streets had also [SOURCE: Page_172]

significantly lower mean ring width (3.21 ± 0.23 mm) than those living in parks (Class 2: 4.50 [SOURCE: Page_172]

169 [SOURCE: Page_172]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_173]

± 0.08 mm and Class 3: 5.18 ± 0.92 mm). Park trees (Class 2 and 3) had also the same mean [SOURCE: Page_173]

ring widths as trees from Class 1 (Street: 4.48 ± 0.55 mm and Park: 4.22 ± 8.26 mm). [SOURCE: Page_173]

Distribution and dynamic of tree reserves [SOURCE: Page_173]

Concentrations in carbohydrates (soluble sugars and starch) were significantly higher [SOURCE: Page_173]

in winter compared to budburst (Fig. 4.2; Table 4.2) suggesting carbohydrates’ storage during [SOURCE: Page_173]

the year 2015 for silver lindens. However, lipids storage process during the growing season [SOURCE: Page_173]

only concerned trees in class 3 with significant differences between concentrations in winter [SOURCE: Page_173]

and budburst and this, regardless of the growing environment (Fig 4.2, Table 4.2: Month x [SOURCE: Page_173]

Class p < 0.05). One week after budburst, total reserves concentrations (lipids and TNSC) [SOURCE: Page_173]

reached between 2.50 to 4.25 % of dry weight for arboretum, park trees and street trees. The [SOURCE: Page_173]

maximum concerned street trees in Class 3 with 4.25 % while the minimum was observed for [SOURCE: Page_173]

trees in Class 1 in parks and streets with 2.5 % (Fig. 4.2). In all groups of trees, lipids could [SOURCE: Page_173]

represent 1.7 to 2.2 % of dry weight when TNSC varied from 0.60 to 2.53 %. In winter, total [SOURCE: Page_173]

reserve concentrations doubled with values between 5 to 9% of dry weight. As in budburst, [SOURCE: Page_173]

the maximum was also observed for street trees in class 3 (9 %) when the lowest total [SOURCE: Page_173]

concentrations were for street trees of Class 1 (4.94 %). Lipids represented from 1.7 to 3.5% [SOURCE: Page_173]

of dry weight when TNSC represented 3.0 % to 5.5 % (Fig.4.2). [SOURCE: Page_173]

In details, carbohydrates seemed to be only stored in sucrose and starch forms. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_173]

glucose and fructose concentrations differences between winter and budburst were around 0 [SOURCE: Page_173]

% and were significantly different from starch, sucrose and lipids storages for all sampled [SOURCE: Page_173]

trees (Table 4.3). So, sucrose form should represent soluble sugars in silver lindens, as TNSC [SOURCE: Page_173]

should represent the addition between starch and sucrose concentrations. [SOURCE: Page_173]

170 [SOURCE: Page_173]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_174]

Growing environment and DBH class impacted significantly starch storage (Table [SOURCE: Page_174]

4.2). Starch stored during 2015 was significantly higher for urban trees in Class 1 in park and [SOURCE: Page_174]

in Class 1 and 2 in streets, with values above 1.5 g 100 g DW-1 than for arboretum trees (0.50 [SOURCE: Page_174]

± 0.15 g 100 g DW-1; Fig. 4.3; Table 4.3). Interestingly, starch storage was not significantly [SOURCE: Page_174]

different between trees in arboretum and Class 1 and 2 in park and 3 in street with stored [SOURCE: Page_174]

concentrations around 1 g 100 g DW-1. Similarly, growing environment significantly [SOURCE: Page_174]

influenced lipids storage (Table 4.2). Lipids stored by arboretum trees were significantly [SOURCE: Page_174]

lower than park or street trees with respectively 0.29 ± 0.18 and 0.45 ± 0.21 g 100 g DW-1 [SOURCE: Page_174]

(average on all urban trees, Table 4.3). Sucrose storage, on the contrary, was independent of [SOURCE: Page_174]

tree age and growing environments (Table 4.2) when DBH class impacted TNSC storage [SOURCE: Page_174]

(Table 4.2, p-value = 0.06) with lowest values for Class 3. [SOURCE: Page_174]

Lipids storage (1.03 ± 0.05 g 100 g DW-1, Table 4.2) and sucrose storage (1.72 ± 0.33 [SOURCE: Page_174]

g 100 g DW-1, Table 4.2) were significantly higher in Class 3 of trees living in street. Younger [SOURCE: Page_174]

trees in Class 1 had higher average starch storage (on average Park: 1.61 ± 0.30 and Street: [SOURCE: Page_174]

1.53 ± 0.18 g 100 g DW-1). Regression analysis (Fig. 4.4) showed that lipid concentrations [SOURCE: Page_174]

were significantly and positively correlated with age (p-value = 0.01, R$_2$= 0.21) when starch [SOURCE: Page_174]

concentration was significantly negatively correlated (p-value = 0.02, R$_2$= 0.17). Regression [SOURCE: Page_174]

analysis showed no correlation with age for sucrose and TNSC (Fig. 4.4). [SOURCE: Page_174]

### 4.3.5. Discussion

Age-specific and site-specific growth response of silver lindens [SOURCE: Page_175]

Our results on urban silver lindens have shown that tree ring width, reflecting mean [SOURCE: Page_175]

annual growth, were larger and well defined in the juvenile phase, considered as a fast- [SOURCE: Page_175]

growing characteristic (Radoglou et al. 2009). Young trees generally present wider and denser [SOURCE: Page_175]

growth rings than older trees (Fritts 1976). [SOURCE: Page_175]

However, trees living in Paris streets older than 50 years had a global lower growth [SOURCE: Page_175]

compared to those with the same age living in parks and arboretum. Other studies showed the [SOURCE: Page_175]

same results on common urban hardwood species with reduced growth compared to rural [SOURCE: Page_175]

conspecifics as a response to several factors or stress (Meyer 1982; Quigley 2004; Roberts et [SOURCE: Page_175]

al. 2006). But opposite trends were also observed (Gregg et al. 2003; Evans 2010). All [SOURCE: Page_175]

together, these controversial results suggest that tree response to urban stress could involve [SOURCE: Page_175]

different factors (Quigley 2004) such as species, urbanization and/or landscape management [SOURCE: Page_175]

of the city, local soil nutrient resources, tree management, as well as climatic factors such as [SOURCE: Page_175]

precipitation, temperature and solar radiation (Schweingruber 1996). However, the limited [SOURCE: Page_175]

growth response of street silver lindens in Paris could be physiologically explained by a [SOURCE: Page_175]

lowest carbon assimilation during photosynthesis due to drought (Clark and Kjelgren 1989; [SOURCE: Page_175]

Sieghardt et al. 2005). Indeed, growth, especially of latewood, is strongly related to soil water [SOURCE: Page_175]

deficit (Hinckley et al. 1976) which lowers turgor pressure in expanding cells. Water deficit [SOURCE: Page_175]

induces stomata closure and, thus, the reduction of carbon uptake through photosynthesis [SOURCE: Page_175]

(Petersen and Eckstein 1988). Street plantations in Paris could be considered as potted plants [SOURCE: Page_175]

with a very limited open-ground surface area, reducing drastically precipitation infiltration. [SOURCE: Page_175]

Furthermore, the important amount of sealing surfaces amplifies local temperatures and water [SOURCE: Page_175]

deficit in the air (Bhaduri et al. 2001; Conway 2007). One major difference for trees living in [SOURCE: Page_175]

172 [SOURCE: Page_175]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_176]

parks and arboretum is the much larger area of unsealed vegetated ground. It induces more [SOURCE: Page_176]

water infiltration and less short term runoff (Barker and Wager 1986; Roberts et al. 2006), [SOURCE: Page_176]

both likely to increase soil water storage. [SOURCE: Page_176]

Distribution and dynamics of tree reserves [SOURCE: Page_176]

In adult trees, the trunk represents the most important biomass (Bartelink 1998; Grulke [SOURCE: Page_176]

and Retzlaff 2001) and thus, could be considered as the largest compartment for C reserves [SOURCE: Page_176]

(Barbaroux and Bréda 2002b). Our results indicated a significant increase of C storage [SOURCE: Page_176]

between budburst and winter for all sampled silver lindens (Fig. 4.2; Table 4.2). Soluble [SOURCE: Page_176]

carbohydrates seem mainly present in sucrose form (Table 4.3, Clair-Maczulajtys and Bory [SOURCE: Page_176]

1988), although it has been reported other possible forms in Tilia spp. such as raffinose [SOURCE: Page_176]

(Jeremias 1968; Höll 1981). Significant starch and lipids in the form of triacylglycerols, [SOURCE: Page_176]

phospholipids and galactolipids (Clair-Maczulajtys and Bory 1988) were also accumulated [SOURCE: Page_176]

through the growing season. These C reserves stored in trunk in winter never exceeded 2.5 % [SOURCE: Page_176]

of the dry weight for starch, 2.5 % for sucrose and 3.5 % for lipids (Fig. 4.3), agreeing with [SOURCE: Page_176]

concentrations found in former researches on Tilia spp. genus (Höll and Priebe 1985; Clair- [SOURCE: Page_176]

Maczulajtys and Bory 1988). [SOURCE: Page_176]

Age-specific characterization [SOURCE: Page_176]

Carbohydrates were accumulated for silver lindens with no significant differences [SOURCE: Page_176]

between age Classes. However, we observed a significant decrease of starch concentration [SOURCE: Page_176]

with age. However, we observed a significant decrease of starch concentration with age (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_176]

4.4) with lower values for Class 3 compared to Class 1. This difference between young and [SOURCE: Page_176]

old trees for starch storage could be due to changes in tree physiology and allocation with [SOURCE: Page_176]

time. Indeed, in contrast to soluble sugars, starch is insoluble in water and therefore [SOURCE: Page_176]

metabolically inactive. Furthermore, starch is composed of glucose polymers allowing [SOURCE: Page_176]

173 [SOURCE: Page_176]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_177]

inexpensive synthesis and rapid degradation. These properties imply that the starch is [SOURCE: Page_177]

generally considered a form of preferential and inexpensive C storage in the short and middle [SOURCE: Page_177]

term (Hoch 2007). [SOURCE: Page_177]

In addition, an opposite trend could be observed for lipids concentrations, as a positive [SOURCE: Page_177]

correlation between lipids and age has been found (Fig 4.4). For younger trees, no [SOURCE: Page_177]

accumulation of lipids during the year, expressed by a stored concentration of around 0% [SOURCE: Page_177]

(Table 4.3), could mean that small triglyceride fractions synthesized during the year would be [SOURCE: Page_177]

directly used in early spring (Höll and Priebe 1985). The hydrolyse products could be [SOURCE: Page_177]

translocated to organs where it is needed such as root, as root growth usually precedes [SOURCE: Page_177]

budbreak (Höll and Priebe 1985). On the contrary for older trees in Class 3, significant lipids [SOURCE: Page_177]

accumulation between budburst and winter is in contradiction with previous researches [SOURCE: Page_177]

showing low or negligible amplitude of seasonal variations in the branches and trunk of four [SOURCE: Page_177]

species of fat trees Tilia, Larix, Picea and Pinus (Nelson and Dickson 1981; Fischer and Holl [SOURCE: Page_177]

1992; Hoch et al. 2003). Actually, it is usually accepted that lipids should be long-term [SOURCE: Page_177]

reserves hardly remobilised during the season. However, in our study, lipids seasonal [SOURCE: Page_177]

variation in Class 3 silver lindens was important, with remobilization in early spring. In [SOURCE: Page_177]

addition, stored lipids represented 1% of the dry matter, at the same level than for starch [SOURCE: Page_177]

(Table 4.3). Furthermore, the increase of storage for lipids associated to the decrease for [SOURCE: Page_177]

starch with age could mean that lipids accumulation occurred at the expense of starch. This [SOURCE: Page_177]

observation has been already made on several coniferous species (Sharkov and Tsvetkova [SOURCE: Page_177]

1950; Karnik et al. 1966; Lebedenko 1966; Jeremias 1968; Sudachkova 1977; Repyakh et al. [SOURCE: Page_177]

1983). Indeed, it has already found that the synthesis and remobilization of lipids are [SOURCE: Page_177]

energetically more expensive than starch, and that these carbon compounds are likely long- [SOURCE: Page_177]

term storage (Hoch et al. 2003). [SOURCE: Page_177]

174 [SOURCE: Page_177]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_178]

Nonetheless, older trees should present increasing demands in water supply compared [SOURCE: Page_178]

to young tree and could be more subjected to water stress. Yoshimura et al. (2016) proposed [SOURCE: Page_178]

that starch under water stress could be converted into soluble sugars. A higher content of [SOURCE: Page_178]

soluble sugars in sapflow could increase the osmotic potential of cells during drought, and [SOURCE: Page_178]

their injection in embolized vessels could ensure their recovery after rainfall (Yoshimura et al. [SOURCE: Page_178]

2016). However, results indicated no significant higher storage of sucrose in older trees and in [SOURCE: Page_178]

urban trees. We propose that a greater sample could help to better determine if this process [SOURCE: Page_178]

occurs in older trees by mitigating intra-individual variability and optimizing statistics. This [SOURCE: Page_178]

explanation might be consistent with increasing evaporative demands and water stress that [SOURCE: Page_178]

larger trees probably face more frequently compared to smaller ones and this, especially in [SOURCE: Page_178]

street. [SOURCE: Page_178]

Site-specific characterization [SOURCE: Page_178]

Starch storage was significantly higher for 50 year-old urban trees than for arboretum [SOURCE: Page_178]

trees of equivalent age but with no significant differences between younger trees of Class 1 in [SOURCE: Page_178]

street and park. In addition, lipids synthesized in arboretum trees were significantly lower [SOURCE: Page_178]

than for park or street trees. So, it seems that trees growing in urban environment increase [SOURCE: Page_178]

their reserves storage in starch and lipids, favouring a middle- and long-term survival when [SOURCE: Page_178]

compared to trees living in well-watered environment (Hoch et al. 2003). These results are in [SOURCE: Page_178]

agreement with studies showing that trees living in high environmental stress such as severe [SOURCE: Page_178]

water stress usually show higher C storage (Hoch et al. 2003; Millard et al. 2007; Gruber et al. [SOURCE: Page_178]

2012a; Simard et al. 2013). [SOURCE: Page_178]

NSC pool relation with growth [SOURCE: Page_178]

The level and changes in both growth and reserves formation could be good [SOURCE: Page_178]

physiological indicators of tree’s health status (Wargo 1975; McCullough and Wagner 1987) [SOURCE: Page_178]

175 [SOURCE: Page_178]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_179]

and it could enable a better understanding of C allocation under water stress effects. The [SOURCE: Page_179]

absence of lipids storage associated with the higher growth for young silver lindens could [SOURCE: Page_179]

imply a greater allocation of their carbon in short-term growth rather than in long-term [SOURCE: Page_179]

storage, making them more sensitive to environmental conditions. This hypothesis was [SOURCE: Page_179]

supported by Genet et al. 2010 who demonstrated two distinct patterns of C allocation due to [SOURCE: Page_179]

age in European beech, a diffuse-porous specie like silver linden. At juvenile stages, [SOURCE: Page_179]

preferential allocation of C to growth takes place, while in the more mature stages the [SOURCE: Page_179]

allocation to growth decreases in favour of storage for reproduction and respiration. [SOURCE: Page_179]

Concerning the older street trees, the increase of reserves storage, especially for lipids [SOURCE: Page_179]

and starch, at the expense of growth seems to indicate that the stress undergone by trees in [SOURCE: Page_179]

streets influences directly the amount of C fixed by trees, as shown by a lower tree ring width [SOURCE: Page_179]

when compared to arboretum trees. However, this stress does not impact directly the amount [SOURCE: Page_179]

of C stored, which increases for lipids and starch storage. It could suggest a trade-off between [SOURCE: Page_179]

short-term growth and survival in the long-term by reserve accumulation (Sala et al. 2012; [SOURCE: Page_179]

Wiley and Helliker 2012) reflecting a change in the allocation of C due to water limitation. [SOURCE: Page_179]

Indeed, previous studies have shown that C allocation to reserves could be prioritized over [SOURCE: Page_179]

growth (Wiley et al. 2013; Saffell et al. 2014). This prioritization of reserves may promote the [SOURCE: Page_179]

long-term survival of trees to more severe and/or more prolonged stress by the maintenance of [SOURCE: Page_179]

vital metabolism (Wiley et al. 2013) through active regulation of reserves accumulation, and [SOURCE: Page_179]

thus growth regulation (Dietze et al. 2014). So, growth could be the main variable of long- [SOURCE: Page_179]

term adjustment of trees under water stress, consistent with the fact that growth is more [SOURCE: Page_179]

sensitive to water stress when compared to other functions (Hsiao 1973) such as respiration or [SOURCE: Page_179]

photosynthesis. [SOURCE: Page_179]

176 [SOURCE: Page_179]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_180]

However, our results did not allow us to conclude about the dynamics and the use of [SOURCE: Page_180]

this significant higher C pool. This pool of C reserve could not be used by urban trees as [SOURCE: Page_180]

previous researches shown a decrease of carbon accessibility in trees under severe stress [SOURCE: Page_180]

(Hoch et al. 2003; Hoch 2005; Bréda et al. 2006; Millard et al. 2007; Palacio et al. 2008; [SOURCE: Page_180]

Gruber et al. 2012b; Simard et al. 2013). This could lead trees to temporary C deficits with a [SOURCE: Page_180]

potential risk of tree decline and mortality (Hoch et al. 2003; Millard et al. 2007; Gruber et al. [SOURCE: Page_180]

2012a) agreeing with high mortality rate faced by street trees. Furthermore, a quantitative [SOURCE: Page_180]

assessment of the NSC seasonal distribution in the entire tree seems important to identify [SOURCE: Page_180]

potential difference in the different organs. It could also help to observe the source-sink [SOURCE: Page_180]

relationships throughout the season. Indeed, once absorbed by leaves, C is transported to sink [SOURCE: Page_180]

organs, where it will be used or stored. When stored, these organs become C sources. [SOURCE: Page_180]

However, as the radial growth is directly dependent on tree water status (Zweifel et al. 2006), [SOURCE: Page_180]

an increase of C reserves in organs related to a decrease in C demands due to water limitation [SOURCE: Page_180]

could occur during a drought (Piper 2011). Hydraulic failure linked to a drought may also [SOURCE: Page_180]

limited supply and isolate sink organs from organs sources, leading to local depletion in [SOURCE: Page_180]

reserves (Sala et al. 2010; McDowell et al. 2011) in roots, for instance. [SOURCE: Page_180]

As other diffuse-porous species, silver lindens are considered more likely to have an [SOURCE: Page_180]

anisohydric behaviour (Radoglou et al. 2009). Smaller xylem vessel should decrease [SOURCE: Page_180]

embolism risk compared to ring-porous species, making them more resilient to low water [SOURCE: Page_180]

potential (Roman et al. 2015). In addition, this ability could increase silver lindens tolerance [SOURCE: Page_180]

during a drought by increasing the threshold of stomata closure. In this case, C assimilation [SOURCE: Page_180]

could be higher than expected and could explain the higher long-term survival reserve [SOURCE: Page_180]

accumulation of lipids and starch despite the chronic and severe water stress encountered by [SOURCE: Page_180]

these trees. Further research on anatomical features such as size or density of xylem vessels [SOURCE: Page_180]

177 [SOURCE: Page_180]

Chapitre 4. Dynamique saisonnière des réserves glucidiques et lipidiques [SOURCE: Page_181]

could help to highlight a likely higher hydraulic efficiency despite possible local C depletions [SOURCE: Page_181]

in urban silver lindens. [SOURCE: Page_181]

### 4.3.6. Conclusions

Our results on urban silver lindens are in agreement with several other studies showing [SOURCE: Page_181]

that trees under water stress could actively regulate their C reserves in a long-term survival [SOURCE: Page_181]

strategy at the expense of short-term radial growth. On the contrary, for younger trees, C [SOURCE: Page_181]

allocation could be mainly attributed to growth. Although all tree reserve compartments have [SOURCE: Page_181]

not been explored, it seems that drought-related reduced growth could be induced by a C [SOURCE: Page_181]

unavailability. This could be caused by a long-term survival strategy aiming the prioritization [SOURCE: Page_181]

of C allocation to reserves accumulation of lipids and starch. It could make trees more [SOURCE: Page_181]

vulnerable in a short-term and increase tree mortality risks under chronic and/or severe water [SOURCE: Page_181]

stress. Under climate change context, drought frequency and severity could increase, [SOURCE: Page_181]

especially in urban areas, and in the case of Paris area. Silver lindens could be exposed to [SOURCE: Page_181]

increased risk of mortality due to regular C deficit in favour of carbon storage. However, [SOURCE: Page_181]

more long-term, multi-species and at the entire individual scale studies are needed to predict [SOURCE: Page_181]

future behaviours of street trees under climate changes. [SOURCE: Page_181]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_205]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]
- Parent_Topic: 1529765
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 5.1. présentation
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
### 5.1. Présentation

Ce dernier article propose de caractériser les principales sources d'eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés plantés en rues et en parcs (urbain) en comparaison à des arbres plantés en arboretum (non-urbain), à deux moments de l'année (printemps et été) à l'aide de la mesure des signatures isotopique de l'eau (G18O et G2H). [SOURCE: Page_205]

Cette étude a été réalisée en collaboration avec Thierry Bariac (DR, CNRS), Philippe Biron (IR) et Patricia Richard (AI) de l’équipe Écogéochimie Isotopique (IsoEco) de l’Institut d’Écologie et des Sciences de l’Environnementale de Paris. [SOURCE: Page_205]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]
- Parent_Topic: 1529765
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 5.2. principaux résultats
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
### 5.2. Principaux résultats

Les résultats indiquent un enrichissement isotopique dû à l'évaporation de l’eau du sol dans les couches supérieures (10-20 cm) des sols urbains, en particulier pendant le mois plus chaud et pour les arbres de la Classe 3. Les phénomènes d’ICU, associés à une diminution de la teneur en eau du sol en raison de la plus forte surface évaporatrice de ces arbres, pourraient rendre les sols urbains plus susceptibles à l'évaporation au cours de l'année. Les principales sources d'eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés semblent provenir du sol en surface, issu d’un mélange entre de l’eau de pluie et de l'eau non potable. Bien qu’ils ne soient soumis à aucune stratégie d'irrigation à Paris, les arbres de rue semblent donc reposer sur l’infiltration de l’eau de pluie dans les sols, mais également sur des entrées ponctuelles d'eau non potable. Des mesures complémentaires en temps réel sur les teneurs en eau des sols permettraient de caractériser l’intensité d’évaporation et la gravité des stress hydriques des arbres plantés en rue à Paris. [SOURCE: Page_205]

### 5.3.1. Abstract

Water sources used by trees living in city streets are still poorly known. In street, trees [SOURCE: Page_207]

may potentially rely on different water sources such as precipitation inputs into soils or non- [SOURCE: Page_207]

potable water networks. However, the dependency to precipitation infiltrations for street trees [SOURCE: Page_207]

could explain both their lower life expectancy and reduced growth due to water limitations [SOURCE: Page_207]

induced by impervious surfaces and higher evaporative demands. In this work, a field study [SOURCE: Page_207]

was conducted on 50 silver lindens, one of the major species planted in streets and parks in [SOURCE: Page_207]

Paris city, France (namely urban growing environments) and compared with individuals [SOURCE: Page_207]

living in arboretum (namely non-urban growing environment). Trees were selected according [SOURCE: Page_207]

to 3 DBH (Diameter at breast height) classes as proxy of age. The isotopic composition (G18O [SOURCE: Page_207]

and G2H) of tree stem water was then compared with those of different potential water sources [SOURCE: Page_207]

(rain, non-potable and soil water at two depths) in spring (May) and summer (August). [SOURCE: Page_207]

Results indicate an isotopic enrichment due to soil evaporation in the shallow depth of urban [SOURCE: Page_207]

soils (layers upper than 20 cm), especially during warmer month and for the largest trees. [SOURCE: Page_207]

Atmospheric urban heat islands phenomena associated with a decrease of soil water content [SOURCE: Page_207]

due to high evaporative demands could make urban soils more likely to undergo evaporation [SOURCE: Page_207]

during the year. Primary water sources for silver lindens seem to come from shallow soils, as [SOURCE: Page_207]

a mixture between rainwater inputs and non-potable water. Although they are not subjected to [SOURCE: Page_207]

any irrigation strategy in Paris, street trees seem to partially rely on punctual water inputs [SOURCE: Page_207]

from non-potable network. Nevertheless, further measurements on the soil water contents [SOURCE: Page_207]

through the year could help to characterize the intensity of soil surface evaporation and the [SOURCE: Page_207]

severity of water stress of the trees planted in streets in Paris city. [SOURCE: Page_207]

Key words: [SOURCE: Page_207]

Urban tree water sources; Ecohydrology; Oxygen 18; Deuterium; Street and park trees [SOURCE: Page_207]

204 [SOURCE: Page_207]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_208]

### 5.3.2. Introduction

Street trees actively participate in the ecology of urban environment due to their [SOURCE: Page_208]

ability to store carbon, their contribution to reduce air pollution and their active role in the [SOURCE: Page_208]

water cycle (Mueller and Day 2005; Nowak et al. 2006; Nowak and Dwyer 2007). However, [SOURCE: Page_208]

the effectiveness of these services depends largely on tree health and survival and so, to their [SOURCE: Page_208]

local abiotic conditions such as water availability. Numerous studies already highlighted the [SOURCE: Page_208]

relation between the high mortality encountered by urban trees and water stress (Whitlow and [SOURCE: Page_208]

Bassuk 1987; Whitlow et al. 1992; Ciais et al. 2005; McCarthy and Pataki 2010; Gillner et al. [SOURCE: Page_208]

2013, 2014, 2015). In addition, it was also shown that this higher mortality concerns [SOURCE: Page_208]

especially younger trees and this, usually within 5 years after their plantation (Lu et al. 2010). [SOURCE: Page_208]

Understanding and predicting tree mortality has become intensively studied in the last [SOURCE: Page_208]

decades in the aim of optimizing their sustainability and services they are providing [SOURCE: Page_208]

(Stephenson et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_208]

In Paris city (France), street trees are usually planted in standard sidewalk pavement [SOURCE: Page_208]

(Grabosky et al. 2001) which exposes them to more radiation, heat and less available water. [SOURCE: Page_208]

Despite this fact, they are not subjected to any particular irrigation strategy, except during the [SOURCE: Page_208]

3 years after plantation. This could imply that street trees face important water stress [SOURCE: Page_208]

compared to trees planted in well-watered environment such as parks and arboretum, making [SOURCE: Page_208]

them highly dependent on precipitation infiltration in soils and whatever other water source [SOURCE: Page_208]

opportunities (Vico et al. 2014) such as non-potable networks. Actually, little is known [SOURCE: Page_208]

concerning the water sources used by street trees, and no information is available concerning [SOURCE: Page_208]

those living in Paris. If we suppose that street trees are highly dependent on soil water coming [SOURCE: Page_208]

from precipitation, the amount and the variability of rainfall among seasons and especially [SOURCE: Page_208]

droughts during summer period, could potentially impact tree functioning. [SOURCE: Page_208]

205 [SOURCE: Page_208]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_209]

Under on-going climate changes, drought may become more common in the future [SOURCE: Page_209]

(Moisselin et al. 2011) especially in Paris region. Higher temperatures will also increase [SOURCE: Page_209]

evapotranspiration demands and a higher intra-annual variability in precipitations could [SOURCE: Page_209]

disturb plant water balance. If street trees rely on different water source opportunities such as [SOURCE: Page_209]

water from adjacent pipes under the pavement or from deeper soil, irrigation could be [SOURCE: Page_209]

unnecessary. However, as a well-established root system is significantly positively correlated [SOURCE: Page_209]

with above-ground plant size (Canadell et al. 1996; Schenk and Jackson 2005), differences [SOURCE: Page_209]

between young and adult trees could also imply different requirements in terms of water [SOURCE: Page_209]

needs and in adapted irrigation practices. Largest trees should have greater probability to [SOURCE: Page_209]

access different water sources than smaller trees (Rozas et al. 2009) with a propensity to [SOURCE: Page_209]

uptake water in deeper soil layers (Bréda et al. 1995; Canadell et al. 1996; Lambers et al. [SOURCE: Page_209]

2008) or colonize pipes. Indeed, roots are able to develop towards the wetter pockets in the [SOURCE: Page_209]

soil, far from dryer sites, due to hydrosensing mechanisms (Darwin 1880; Takahashi and [SOURCE: Page_209]

Scott 1993; Hirasawa et al. 1997; Tsuda et al. 2003). [SOURCE: Page_209]

In ecohydrosystems, the use of water heavy isotopes (18O and 2H) has proved to be [SOURCE: Page_209]

relevant for the identification and analysis of the origins and history of water (Durand et al. [SOURCE: Page_209]

2007; Werner et al. 2012). For a given flux, different physical, chemical or biological [SOURCE: Page_209]

processes imprint the isotopic composition of water (i.e. isotopic fractionation). Each process [SOURCE: Page_209]

can be thus back-traced if the changes in isotopic compositions induced by all processes are [SOURCE: Page_209]

known. As no fractionation happens during root uptake (Zimmermann et al. 1967; Ellsworth [SOURCE: Page_209]

and Williams 2007), stem water should represent the homogenization of different signatures [SOURCE: Page_209]

of the water sources absorbed by the roots at different depths. In urban area and street soil, [SOURCE: Page_209]

water should be supply by rain and/or from non-potable water (Morrison et al. 2001; Braud et [SOURCE: Page_209]

al. 2005; Bijoor et al. 2012). Thus, isotopic variation of xylem water should be due to 1) [SOURCE: Page_209]

variation in the isotopic composition of precipitation, 2) by the more or less complete mixing [SOURCE: Page_209]

206 [SOURCE: Page_209]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_210]

of different precipitation signatures in soil and then 3) by fractionation process in soil due to [SOURCE: Page_210]

evaporation and causing an isotopic enrichment of soil water related to more rapid [SOURCE: Page_210]

vaporization of the lighter isotopes. This evaporation usually typically occurs within the first [SOURCE: Page_210]

10 to 20 cm of soil, and rarely beyond (Michener and Lajtha 2007) although it could also [SOURCE: Page_210]

depend on climate or soil type, rain variations and evaporative demands. [SOURCE: Page_210]

This study proposes to characterize the main water sources used by silver lindens, a [SOURCE: Page_210]

predominant species planted in streets and parks in Paris (France), in spring and summer [SOURCE: Page_210]

using water isotopic characterization (G18O and G2H). Stem water isotopic composition was [SOURCE: Page_210]

compared to 4 different potential water sources namely: rainwater, non-potable water [SOURCE: Page_210]

(considered as deep water) and soil water at 10-20 and 30-40 cm depths. In order to highlight [SOURCE: Page_210]

differences in water uptake between young and adult trees due to root establishment and [SOURCE: Page_210]

potential different water needs, sampled trees in streets and parks were selected among three [SOURCE: Page_210]

Diameter at Breast Height (DBH) classes used as a proxy of tree age. Then, the water sources [SOURCE: Page_210]

potentially used by street silver lindens were compared to the water sources used by trees [SOURCE: Page_210]

living in park and arboretum. We assume that, because of lower water availability in streets [SOURCE: Page_210]

due to impervious soils, water sources could be different due to a change in their water uptake [SOURCE: Page_210]

strategies, non-potable water opportunities or a strongest fractionation due to higher urban [SOURCE: Page_210]

soil evaporation. [SOURCE: Page_210]

207 [SOURCE: Page_210]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_211]
