### 5.3.3. Methods

Sampling design [SOURCE: Page_211]

This study was conducted in Paris city, France (48,8534N; 2,3488E). Parisian climate [SOURCE: Page_211]

is temperate, sub-Atlantic (Crippa et al. 2013). During the studied year 2015, mean [SOURCE: Page_211]

temperature was of 13°C, and reached for the coldest months 5°C in January and February [SOURCE: Page_211]

respectively, while for the warmer months in July and August, it reached 22°C and 21°C. [SOURCE: Page_211]

Sum of annual precipitations reached 507 mm, with a maximum in August and September (80 [SOURCE: Page_211]

and 72 mm respectively) and a minimum in July and December with values of 52 and 22 mm, [SOURCE: Page_211]

respectively (Montsouris meteorological station (Paris) 48.8566N; 2.3366E, Météo France [SOURCE: Page_211]

station 07156). [SOURCE: Page_211]

Paris city is currently one of the most wooded capitals in Europe with more than 490 [SOURCE: Page_211]

000 trees in 2010 (parks, gardens, schools, streets) including more than 100 000 planted in [SOURCE: Page_211]

alignment (Apur 2010b). Tilia tomentosa Moench is one of the four predominant species [SOURCE: Page_211]

planted in the streets of Paris (Apur 2010b). Indeed, lime trees are known to be very resistant [SOURCE: Page_211]

to biotic and abiotic stress (Ţenche-Constantinescu et al. 2015) and usually grow successfully [SOURCE: Page_211]

in warmer and dryer conditions (Sjöman and Oprea 2010). In the last known inventory of [SOURCE: Page_211]

2010, 5724 individuals in 111 different streets in Paris were identified, representing more than [SOURCE: Page_211]

6% of the total street trees (Apur 2010b). We selected 50 individuals for isotopic analysis. [SOURCE: Page_211]

This study consisted in a multiple sites sampling, through suburban and urban areas in [SOURCE: Page_211]

Paris region, to take into account spatial variability of water sources potentially used by urban [SOURCE: Page_211]

trees. We selected 50 individuals for isotopic analysis in three different growing [SOURCE: Page_211]

environments: 30 within Paris streets, 15 in Paris parks and 5 in the Arboretum of Chèvreloup [SOURCE: Page_211]

(Museum National d’Histoire Naturelle (MNHN), Rocquencourt, France; Fig. 5.1). Street and [SOURCE: Page_211]

208 [SOURCE: Page_211]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_212]

park sites were identified as urban growing environment while Arboretum corresponded to [SOURCE: Page_212]

non-urban, suburban growing environment. The health status of each tree was visually [SOURCE: Page_212]

assessed according to a visual tree assessment (VTA) protocol (Mattheck and Breloer 1994) [SOURCE: Page_212]

and only vigorous trees were selected. The 45 urban and 5 suburban chosen trees were [SOURCE: Page_212]

grouped according to 3 classes of DBH. The DBH was preliminary used as a proxy of tree age [SOURCE: Page_212]

because no information, such as plantation date, was available. The age of each tree and ring- [SOURCE: Page_212]

width was determined by using wood core samples and dendrochronological methods fully [SOURCE: Page_212]

explained in a previous study (David et al. 2016). Class 1 included trees with DBH comprised [SOURCE: Page_212]

between 6 and 15 cm (young trees), Class 2 with DBH between 32 and 43 cm (young adults), [SOURCE: Page_212]

and Class 3 with DBH between 56 and 74 cm (adults). Total tree height and crown height [SOURCE: Page_212]

were measured with an electronic clinometer (Haglöf EC II). The DBH and canopy spread in [SOURCE: Page_212]

four directions were measured using a measuring tape. Crown volume was estimated using [SOURCE: Page_212]

the ellipsoid formula for volume. [SOURCE: Page_212]

Street and parks sites in Paris were selected because of the concomitant presence of [SOURCE: Page_212]

silver lindens within the three classes of DBH. Due to their close geographical location, street [SOURCE: Page_212]

and park sites shared similitude in their management. When a new tree is planted, a pit of 10 [SOURCE: Page_212]

m^3 on average is dug and filled with imported soil from surrounding suburban agricultural [SOURCE: Page_212]

areas. Plantations are then irrigated with 250 L of water every two weeks and during three [SOURCE: Page_212]

years. Afterwards, there is no management practice other than pruning, and no fertilizer is [SOURCE: Page_212]

added (Pellegrini 2012; Mairie de Paris 2016). Street trees were selected among 14 different [SOURCE: Page_212]

streets. They were distributed in 9 arrondissements (i.e. administrative districts) (Fig. 5.1). For [SOURCE: Page_212]

each street, only trees in standard sidewalk and with bare or drain-covered soils were selected [SOURCE: Page_212]

to avoid important differences in terms of rooting conditions and water availability (Rahman [SOURCE: Page_212]

et al. 2011). Parks trees were from 6 parks or open spaces (Fig. 5.1), built from the second [SOURCE: Page_212]

part of the 19th century to the second part of the 20th century. In the Arboretum of Chèvreloup, [SOURCE: Page_212]

209 [SOURCE: Page_212]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_213]

only trees from Class 3 were present. Samplings were made in 2015 in May and August. May, [SOURCE: Page_213]

a generally wet month, corresponds to the beginning of the growing season for silver lindens [SOURCE: Page_213]

in Paris while August, with generally warmer temperatures and lower precipitation, represents [SOURCE: Page_213]

the most likely month for water deficits and soil evaporation. [SOURCE: Page_213]

Stem, soil sampling and water extraction [SOURCE: Page_213]

At each sampling, we collected woody non-green stem (xylem) samples about 5 cm [SOURCE: Page_213]

long and 4 to 9 mm in diameter. The bark of stems was removed to eliminate the possibility [SOURCE: Page_213]

of enriched water near the bark due to evaporation. In addition, soil was sampled on two [SOURCE: Page_213]

points with a 2 cm diameter gouge hand auger, and the 10-20 and 30-40 cm layers from each [SOURCE: Page_213]

extracted core were respectively pooled. Sampling distance from trunk was between 20 and [SOURCE: Page_213]

40 cm, depending on the accessibility (size of drain-covered holes or obstruction by thick [SOURCE: Page_213]

roots). Samples were stored in dark airtight vials and directly frozen. During the weeks of [SOURCE: Page_213]

sampling field, no precipitation occurred, limiting bias in soil water isotopic composition due [SOURCE: Page_213]

to new inputs of fresh rainwater. The water extraction of soil and stem samples for isotopic [SOURCE: Page_213]

analysis was then conducted by cryogenic vacuum distillation (Araguás-Araguás et al. 1995; [SOURCE: Page_213]

West et al. 2006). In the case of an incomplete distillation, the results were corrected with the [SOURCE: Page_213]

model of Rayleigh. [SOURCE: Page_213]

Rain and non-potable water sources [SOURCE: Page_213]

Rain water was sampled according to a technical procedure for cumulative monthly [SOURCE: Page_213]

sampling of precipitation for isotopic analyses determined by the International Atomic Energy [SOURCE: Page_213]

Agency and the World Meteorological Organization (2013). On May and August, on the first [SOURCE: Page_213]

day and until the last day of the month, rain collectors were putted in streets plantation sites [SOURCE: Page_213]

and settled up 1 m above crown of trees with a rod in order to prevent possible vandalism. [SOURCE: Page_213]

210 [SOURCE: Page_213]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_214]

Each sample represents the integrated precipitation for a one-month period. In May 10 rain [SOURCE: Page_214]

collectors were settled while in August 15 collectors were installed to prevent possible [SOURCE: Page_214]

vandalism (Fig. 5.1). At each sampling, non-potable water was also collected directly from [SOURCE: Page_214]

gutter taps in street and park plantation sites, stored in dark airtight vials and directly frozen. [SOURCE: Page_214]

Isotope analysis [SOURCE: Page_214]

18 O water analyses were performed by CO$_2$ equilibration (Epstein and Mayeda, 1953) [SOURCE: Page_214]

using isotope ratio mass spectrometer (IRMS, Aquaprep, Micromass: Isoprime coupled to a [SOURCE: Page_214]

Gilson X222). The measures of 2H were produced by pyrolysis of the water molecule on a [SOURCE: Page_214]

chrome reactor with continuous flow of ultra-pure helium using an IRMS (PyrOH: Isoprime) [SOURCE: Page_214]

coupled to an elemental analyzer (EuroVector) (Morrison et al. 2001). The measured values [SOURCE: Page_214]

were calibrated against internal standards from laboratory, themselves calibrated against [SOURCE: Page_214]

international standards. The results were then normalized with theoretical values of [SOURCE: Page_214]

international standards VSMOW 2 (Vienna Standard Mean Ocean Water) and SLAP-2 [SOURCE: Page_214]

(Standard Light Antarctic Precipitation). Measurement standard errors were respectively [SOURCE: Page_214]

0.15‰ and 0.8‰ for 18O and 2H. Oxygen and hydrogen isotope measurements were [SOURCE: Page_214]

expressed inG notation calculated as follow (eq. 1): [SOURCE: Page_214]

𝑅𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 𝜕 2𝐻 𝑜𝑟 𝜕 18𝑂 = (           − 1) × 1000        (𝑒𝑞. 1) 𝑅𝑆𝑀𝑂𝑊 [SOURCE: Page_214]

with Rsample: the ratio of heavy molecules on the total number of molecules in the sample and [SOURCE: Page_214]

RSMOW: ratio report in an international reference (ie. SMOW). [SOURCE: Page_214]

211 [SOURCE: Page_214]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_215]

Statistical analysis [SOURCE: Page_215]

Statistical analyses were performed using R 3.2.0 (R Development Core Team 2011). [SOURCE: Page_215]

The effects of sites and DBH class were then analyzed. DBH, height, age and crown volume [SOURCE: Page_215]

differences between class and living environment were tested using ANOVA and Tukey post- [SOURCE: Page_215]

hoc. Differences between month, DBH classes and growing environment for stem water and [SOURCE: Page_215]

soil water isotope values were statistically tested using a temporal pseudoreplication analysis [SOURCE: Page_215]

performed by mixed effect models with tree as a random effect. These models where then [SOURCE: Page_215]

subjected to Tukey contrasts post-hoc tests. Mann-Whitney tests were also performed to [SOURCE: Page_215]

compare isotopic composition between non-potable and rain water according to month. [SOURCE: Page_215]

Differences were considered significant at p < 0.05. [SOURCE: Page_215]

Water sources determination [SOURCE: Page_215]

Global meteoric water line (GMWL, Craig 1961) was used to estimate local rainwater [SOURCE: Page_215]

deviation in May and August. Then, a local meteoric water line was constructed using least [SOURCE: Page_215]

square regression of the isotopic composition of rainwater in both May and August. Local [SOURCE: Page_215]

rainwater and non-potable water deviations from GMWL and local meteoric water line [SOURCE: Page_215]

(LMWL) were respectively estimated by implementing average values of G18O and G2H in [SOURCE: Page_215]

these equations. [SOURCE: Page_215]

Water in shallow soils may be isotopically enriched when compared to deeper soil [SOURCE: Page_215]

water due to evaporation (Busch et al. 1992; Brunel et al. 1995). Evaporation lines in a G18O [SOURCE: Page_215]

and G2H diagram (Craig 1961) were constructed using a least square linear regression of the [SOURCE: Page_215]

isotopic composition of soil water at both depths (Gat and Matsui 1991; Gat 1996). As [SOURCE: Page_215]

evaporative enrichment decreases with depth (Allison and Hughes 1983) and typically occurs [SOURCE: Page_215]

within the first 10 to 20 cm of soil (Michener and Lajtha 2007), we assume that the soil [SOURCE: Page_215]

212 [SOURCE: Page_215]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_216]

evaporation line using measurement in the top of 0-40 cm was representative of the entire soil [SOURCE: Page_216]

isotopic profile (Allison 1982; Allison and Hughes 1983; Bijoor et al. 2012). To establish the [SOURCE: Page_216]

soil water source, we determined if the isotopic composition of non-potable water from pipes [SOURCE: Page_216]

fell within the standard error (SE) of the soil evaporation line (Bijoor et al. 2012). When this [SOURCE: Page_216]

was the case, we concluded that soil water was also composed of this evaporated water source [SOURCE: Page_216]

in addition to rainwater. [SOURCE: Page_216]

Stem water was considered to be a mixture between soil water, rainwater and non- [SOURCE: Page_216]

potable water. Groundwater, in layers deeper than 20 m in Paris (Lamé 2013), was not [SOURCE: Page_216]

considered as a relevant water source in this study as urban soil is made up of thick layer of [SOURCE: Page_216]

old constructions and ballasts (Rossignol 2013) limiting the rise of groundwater by capillarity. [SOURCE: Page_216]

In addition, groundwater is supplied by rainwater (Clark and Fritz 1997). This supply remains [SOURCE: Page_216]

still very limited under the effect of urbanization and important impervious surfaces (Lamé [SOURCE: Page_216]

2013). Tree water sources were identified using 2-3 mixing model approach (Phillips and [SOURCE: Page_216]

Gregg 2001). Bayesian mixing models are a useful tool for inferring the relative contributions [SOURCE: Page_216]

of multiple different source items to a mixture. The proportions of the different water sources [SOURCE: Page_216]

in the isotopic signature of plant xylem water were quantified using the Bayesian isotopic [SOURCE: Page_216]

mixing model performed by the simmr packages developed by Parnell in 2016. The model is [SOURCE: Page_216]

fitted using standard Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) with Metropolis-Hastings steps to [SOURCE: Page_216]

update the source proportions. MCMC works by repeatedly guessing the values of the sources [SOURCE: Page_216]

proportions and find those values which fit the data best (Parnell et al. 2013; Parnell 2016). [SOURCE: Page_216]

213 [SOURCE: Page_216]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_217]

### 5.3.4. Results

Tree characteristics [SOURCE: Page_217]

Trees in Class 3 had higher DBH (Arboretum: 71.90 ± 11.51 cm (mean ± SE); Street: [SOURCE: Page_217]

62.70 ± 2.71 cm; Park: 60. 60 ± 2.50 cm) when Class 2 had intermediate values (Park: 41.00 [SOURCE: Page_217]

± 2.50 cm and Street: 37.92 ± 0.98 cm) and Class 1 lower values (Park: 12.84 ± 1.88 cm and [SOURCE: Page_217]

Street: 12.90 ± 1.00 cm; Table 5.1). The height of trees differed between DBH classes. The [SOURCE: Page_217]

highest trees were from Class 3 with 18.40 ± 0.94 m, 19.42 ± 1.60 m and 23.34 ± 2.99 m in [SOURCE: Page_217]

street, park and arboretum respectively. Intermediate heights were found in trees from Class 2 [SOURCE: Page_217]

in street and park (Street: 13.26 ± 0.77 m; Park: 15.10 ± 2.32 m) but were not significantly [SOURCE: Page_217]

different from trees in Class 3 in the same living environments. The smallest trees were from [SOURCE: Page_217]

Class 1 with 6.42 ± 0.71 and 7.50 ± 0.54 m in park and street respectively. As expected, the [SOURCE: Page_217]

lowest crown volumes were observed in Class 1 (Street: 0.21 ± 0.02 m^3 and Park: 0.19 ± 0.04 [SOURCE: Page_217]

m^3; Table 5.1) when the largest ones were in Class 3 (Street: 1.43 ± 0.14 m^3; Park: 2.32 ± [SOURCE: Page_217]

0.53 m^3 and Arboretum: 2.72 ± 0.60 m^3). The oldest trees were observed from Class 3 in [SOURCE: Page_217]

street (82 ± 2 y; Table 5.1), and had the same age as trees from Class 3 in arboretum (68 ± 12 [SOURCE: Page_217]

y). Trees from Class 2 in streets, and Class 2 and 3 in parks had intermediate ages with [SOURCE: Page_217]

respectively 50 ± 1; 40 ± 8 and 48 ± 7 y. Younger trees were from Class 1 with 13 ± 1 and 12 [SOURCE: Page_217]

± 2 y in streets and parks, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_217]

Isotopic composition of rainwater and non-potable water [SOURCE: Page_217]

In 2015, rain samples collected in May were significantly enriched by 3.88 ± 0.29 ‰ [SOURCE: Page_217]

for G18O and 31.27 ± 2.01 ‰ for G2H (Fig. 5.2) relatively to August rain samples (Mann- [SOURCE: Page_217]

Whitney: p < 0.05, W= 90 forG18O and p < 0.05, W= 90 for G2H). Local Meteoric Water Line [SOURCE: Page_217]

(LMWL) in Paris was defined by the equation (eq. 2) (p < 0.05, R$_2$ = 0.99): [SOURCE: Page_217]

214 [SOURCE: Page_217]

between depths. Soil water of arboretum trees in May did not deviate from the LMWL while [SOURCE: Page_219]

in August, soil water slightly fell to the right of the LMWL (Fig. 3), suggesting little [SOURCE: Page_219]

evaporation at shallow depth (10-20 cm) but with no significant differences between depths. [SOURCE: Page_219]

Non-potable water fell within the SE of soil evaporation lines for both May and [SOURCE: Page_219]

only in May for Class 3 (Fig. 5.3). One exception concerned trees from Class 3 in streets in [SOURCE: Page_219]

Isotopic composition of stem water [SOURCE: Page_219]

The stem water of urban silver lindens in parks and streets was significantly enriched [SOURCE: Page_219]

in May compared to August by 0.68 ± 0.11 ‰ for G18O and 4.90 ± 0.54 ‰ for G2H (p < 0.05, [SOURCE: Page_219]

Table 5.2). No difference was found for trees growing in the arboretum. The growing [SOURCE: Page_219]

environment and DBH classes did not impact the isotopic composition of stem water, except [SOURCE: Page_219]

in May for street and arboretum trees. In this case, G18O was significantly higher by 0.82 ± [SOURCE: Page_219]

0.12 ‰ for street trees relative to the arboretum. [SOURCE: Page_219]

In May, the average stem water of arboretum trees was close to soil isotopic [SOURCE: Page_219]

composition at 10-20 cm (Fig. 5.3.A). The Bayesian isotopic mixing model estimated the [SOURCE: Page_219]

isotopic composition of arboretum trees as a mixture, with mainly 42 % of shallow soil water [SOURCE: Page_219]

from 10-20 cm, 40% from deeper soil in 30-40 cm depth (Table 5.3). In parks, silver linden [SOURCE: Page_219]

water sources seemed to differ with DBH class. For trees in Class 1, isotopic composition [SOURCE: Page_219]

mixture of stem water was close to soil water in the 10-20 cm layer (Fig. 5.3.C, Table 5.3: [SOURCE: Page_219]

29.7 %) and non-potable water (23%, Table 5.3). In Class 2, stem water isotopic composition [SOURCE: Page_219]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_220]

was close to that of shallow soil water (Fig. 5.3.E) and consisted in a mixture of water mainly [SOURCE: Page_220]

from shallow soil layer (10-20 cm, 33%), deeper soil layer (30-40 cm, 30%) and from non- [SOURCE: Page_220]

potable water (29.1%). In Class 3, stem water was isotopically close to the water from the [SOURCE: Page_220]

shallow soil layer (Fig. 5.3.G) and consisted in a mixture of non-potable water, water from the [SOURCE: Page_220]

shallow soil layer and from deeper soil layer. In streets, water sources of younger silver [SOURCE: Page_220]

lindens in Class 1 was mainly composed of water from the shallow soil layer (37%) and non- [SOURCE: Page_220]

potable water (20%, Fig. 5.3.I), while in Class 2, 35.7% was from water in the deeper soil [SOURCE: Page_220]

layer, 24.6% from non-potable water and 22.5% from the shallow soil layer (Fig. 5.3.K, Table [SOURCE: Page_220]

5.3). For silver lindens in Class 3, the isotopic composition of stem water was from the [SOURCE: Page_220]

shallow soil layer, deeper soil layer and non-potable water (Fig. 5.3.M, Table 5.3). [SOURCE: Page_220]

In August, arboretum trees water sources were composed mainly of shallow soil water [SOURCE: Page_220]

(69.7%) with a small fraction from deeper soil (12%, Fig. 5.3.B., Table 3). As non-potable [SOURCE: Page_220]

water and rain isotopic composition were not significantly different in August, the source [SOURCE: Page_220]

could be considered as a combination of rain and non-potable water and thus, should be the [SOURCE: Page_220]

major water sources with water from shallow soil for urban trees. I In parks, water sources of [SOURCE: Page_220]

younger silver lindens in Class 1 were from non-potable water (29.5%) and from deeper soil [SOURCE: Page_220]

(28.3%, Fig. 5.3.D, Table 5.3). In Classes 2 and 3 as for street trees, water sources mixture of [SOURCE: Page_220]

silver lindens was mainly from shallow soil and non-potable water (Figs. 3.F.H.L.N, Table [SOURCE: Page_220]

5.3). [SOURCE: Page_220]

### 5.3.5. Discussion

Isotopic composition of water sources [SOURCE: Page_221]

The LMWL in Paris stayed close to the GMWL with a slope lightly less than 8 (7.90). [SOURCE: Page_221]

Usually, isotopic values of summer precipitation are found more enriched over the season [SOURCE: Page_221]

(Michener and Lajtha 2007). Despite this fact, in May 2015 in Paris, rainwater was enriched [SOURCE: Page_221]

relatively to August precipitation. This could be likely due to the excess of precipitation in [SOURCE: Page_221]

August 2015 with accumulations well above normal levels of 1.5-3 times (MétéoFrance [SOURCE: Page_221]

2016). In addition, in May 2015, the rainfall deficit was over 30 % compared to seasonal [SOURCE: Page_221]

norms (MétéoFrance 2016). As heavy isotopes will fall first during a rainfall (Ladouche et al. [SOURCE: Page_221]

2001), the isotopic composition of rain clouds will be lighter through time (Michener and [SOURCE: Page_221]

Lajtha 2007). This could explain the depletion of heavy isotope we observed in rainwater of [SOURCE: Page_221]

August 2015 as more precipitation fell. This isotopic enrichment in May of rain water due to [SOURCE: Page_221]

the amount effect (Michener and Lajtha 2007) could likely also explain the significant [SOURCE: Page_221]

difference found with non-potable water when in August, isotopic composition of rain and [SOURCE: Page_221]

non-potable water stayed quite similar. In Paris, non-potable water comes from the Seine [SOURCE: Page_221]

River. As surface waters may derive quite directly from rainfall (Darling et al. 2003), isotopic [SOURCE: Page_221]

signatures of rain water and non-potable water are usually equivalent. [SOURCE: Page_221]

Isotopic composition of soil water [SOURCE: Page_221]

Isotopic variations in plant water come from isotopic variations in precipitation, depth [SOURCE: Page_221]

of root extraction, soil water and particularly from the isotopic enrichment due to evaporation [SOURCE: Page_221]

(Dawson et al. 2002). Usually, soil water isotopic composition comes from rainwater and [SOURCE: Page_221]

groundwater due to capillarity rise (Bréda et al. 1995). Soil water could be subjected to [SOURCE: Page_221]

evaporation in upper layers (Shi et al. 2012) and in summer (Michener and Lajtha 2007). As [SOURCE: Page_221]

218 [SOURCE: Page_221]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_222]

no rain fell during sampling, the shallow soil layer (10-20 cm) is likely to be more subjected [SOURCE: Page_222]

to evaporation with more enriched isotopic composition than deeper soil. However, the [SOURCE: Page_222]

intensity of soil evaporation differs with the season, growing environment and aboveground [SOURCE: Page_222]

tree size. [SOURCE: Page_222]

First, no significant enrichment in shallow soil was found for arboretum trees through [SOURCE: Page_222]

the season, suggesting lower soil evaporation than in urban areas, even during summer. [SOURCE: Page_222]

Second, in May, a significant enrichment of the shallow soil layer relative to the deeper layer [SOURCE: Page_222]

was only found for the largest silver lindens (Class 3) living in streets. Third, in August, [SOURCE: Page_222]

significant soil evaporation in surface was observed for silver lindens in street regardless their [SOURCE: Page_222]

DBH classes, and for the largest silver lindens (Class 3) in parks. Those results should [SOURCE: Page_222]

indicate that peculiarities of urban microclimate, soil, and tree size could promote soil [SOURCE: Page_222]

evaporation. On the one hand, it is well known that urban areas are subjected to the urban heat [SOURCE: Page_222]

island (UHI). UHI characteristics associate an increase in air temperature and vapor deficit, [SOURCE: Page_222]

both factors related to soil evaporation. Shi et al. (2012) revealed an obvious impact of the [SOURCE: Page_222]

heat island effect on urban soil with soil temperature usually higher than rural soil (+ 2.02°C [SOURCE: Page_222]

on average). Thus, atmospheric UHI could lead to a soil UHI (Shi et al. 2012). Moreover, [SOURCE: Page_222]

they also found that the heat island effect on urban soils is associated to a dry island effect [SOURCE: Page_222]

with soil moisture content generally lower than that of rural soil. Also highlighted by our [SOURCE: Page_222]

study, this intensity could vary through the year with higher values for summer months and [SOURCE: Page_222]

affecting especially shallow urban soil. On the other hand, precipitation will interact more [SOURCE: Page_222]

with canopy of largest trees, with the enrichment of the isotopic signal of precipitation [SOURCE: Page_222]

reaching the ground by the evaporation of intercepted water (Michener and Lajtha 2007). [SOURCE: Page_222]

Overall, larger trees have also higher water uptake due to higher evaporative demands [SOURCE: Page_222]

inducing a decrease in soil water content. So, in synergy with the urban heat island effect and [SOURCE: Page_222]

219 [SOURCE: Page_222]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_223]

the dry island effect, largest trees could promote soil evaporation as soil specific heat and [SOURCE: Page_223]

volumetric heat capacity decrease with decreasing moisture content (Abu-Hamdeh 2003). [SOURCE: Page_223]

Non-potable water fell within the SE of soil evaporation line of urban trees in May and [SOURCE: Page_223]

August but one exception was found for street trees in Class 3 in August. This overall result [SOURCE: Page_223]

indicates that soil water was a mixture between rainwater and non-potable water more or less [SOURCE: Page_223]

evaporated according to growing environment, month or tree size, as suggested before. As [SOURCE: Page_223]

street trees are not usually irrigated (except for the 3 first years after planting), the non- [SOURCE: Page_223]

potable water sources found in street soil could be due to punctual inputs or leaks in non- [SOURCE: Page_223]

potable network. Almost all of the distributed non-potable water is consumed by municipal [SOURCE: Page_223]

services for watering green spaces and washing pavement and sewers. In addition and [SOURCE: Page_223]

according to Safege (2008), potential leaks in the non-potable network could be around 100 [SOURCE: Page_223]

000 m^3/d in Paris, although those estimations are still controversial (Apur 2010a). Opposed to [SOURCE: Page_223]

street silver lindens of other classes, soil water of street trees in Class 3 did not seem to [SOURCE: Page_223]

include non-potable water. Thus, soil water isotopic composition seems in that case to be only [SOURCE: Page_223]

from more or less evaporated rainwater although the stem water composition overlaps non- [SOURCE: Page_223]

potable water isotopic composition (Fig. 5.3.N). This could mean that, especially for the [SOURCE: Page_223]

largest street trees in warmer months, another water source could directly come from non- [SOURCE: Page_223]

potable network with no preliminary mixing and evaporation. This unforeseen result is [SOURCE: Page_223]

nevertheless consistent with the observation of roots following non-potable pipes under the [SOURCE: Page_223]

pavement due to hydrosensing (personal observation and personal communication from city [SOURCE: Page_223]

foresters of Mairie de Paris). [SOURCE: Page_223]

220 [SOURCE: Page_223]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_224]

Isotopic composition of stem water and potential water sources [SOURCE: Page_224]

When compared to urban trees, silver lindens living and growing in arboretum in our [SOURCE: Page_224]

study revealed that stem water composition was similar (May) or slightly enriched (August) [SOURCE: Page_224]

compared to soil water content in shallow soils (Fig. 5.3). Thus, silver linden in suitable soil [SOURCE: Page_224]

conditions seems to mainly uptake water in the upper layer of the soil, directly supplied by [SOURCE: Page_224]

rainwater and this, regardless of the month considered. Usually, as long as the most [SOURCE: Page_224]

superficial layers of soil have adequate moisture, the plants tend to absorb most of their water [SOURCE: Page_224]

in upper layers of soil where the roots are concentrated (Lambers et al. 2008). However, this [SOURCE: Page_224]

could also suggest that silver lindens have specific shallow root architecture. Root depth and [SOURCE: Page_224]

spread can be highly limited in urban area (Day et al. 2010). Recent studies have shown that a [SOURCE: Page_224]

slight increase in soil compaction might negatively impact root growth (Kormanek et al. [SOURCE: Page_224]

2015). Predicting architectural root systems behavior in urban conditions remains difficult as [SOURCE: Page_224]

many factors could be involved e.g. species specificity, soil texture, soil compaction and [SOURCE: Page_224]

obstacles (Day et al. 2010). However, Grabosky et al in 2001 found a drastic reduction of root [SOURCE: Page_224]

growth for Tilia cordata planted in standard sidewalk when compared to other soils (namely [SOURCE: Page_224]

in the study: agricultural field or skeletal soil material) with a root depth limited to the upper [SOURCE: Page_224]

20 cm. [SOURCE: Page_224]

In our study, for urban silver lindens in parks and streets in May and August, the isotopic [SOURCE: Page_224]

composition of stem water was systematically above the soil evaporation line meaning a [SOURCE: Page_224]

slight enrichment relatively to soil water. A primary water source for those trees seems to [SOURCE: Page_224]

mainly come from shallow soil layers (10-20 cm). However, isotopic composition of soil [SOURCE: Page_224]

water was, in the regard of the month, a more or less evaporated mixture between rainwater [SOURCE: Page_224]

and non-potable water. This result seems to confirm that silver lindens in park and street [SOURCE: Page_224]

mainly rely on both rainwater and non-potable water sources. As street silver lindens are not [SOURCE: Page_224]

221 [SOURCE: Page_224]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_225]

regularly irrigated contrary to trees in park subjected to automatic watering system, it appears [SOURCE: Page_225]

that they could take advantage of 1) punctual inputs of non-potable water due to washing of [SOURCE: Page_225]

the sidewalks and pavement, as well as sewer cleaning and/or 2) unlimited access through [SOURCE: Page_225]

potential leaks in the non-potable network. Despite this fact, it is difficult to accurately [SOURCE: Page_225]

determine whether non-potable water sources are punctual inputs or a leak. In the latter case, [SOURCE: Page_225]

it could provide very large amount of water for trees, preventing them from water stress and [SOURCE: Page_225]

imply unnecessary irrigation due to the access of a potential unlimited non-potable water [SOURCE: Page_225]

sources. However, a previous study made on those silver lindens trees suggests strong water [SOURCE: Page_225]

stresses with drastically reduced growth (David et al. 2016). So, it appears that water uptake [SOURCE: Page_225]

from the non-potable water network should only be punctual through the year. Nevertheless, [SOURCE: Page_225]

it was generally observed that trees with an isotopic composition in their xylem similar to the [SOURCE: Page_225]

water in deep soil layers suffer less water stress and have higher transpiration rate and lower [SOURCE: Page_225]

efficiency use, compared to species with a similar isotopic signature of shallow soil water [SOURCE: Page_225]

(Lambers et al. 2008). [SOURCE: Page_225]

Finally, as we assume no evaporation occurs under 40 cm depth, the isotopic [SOURCE: Page_225]

compositions of the entire soil profile and groundwater have not been measured in our study. [SOURCE: Page_225]

It remains possible that silver linden roots could uptake a small proportion of water from [SOURCE: Page_225]

deeper soil layers (Canadell et al. 1996; Schenk and Jackson 2005) and from groundwater [SOURCE: Page_225]

(Bréda et al. 1995; Bijoor et al. 2012). However in urban soils, soil under 90 cm depth in the [SOURCE: Page_225]

pit plantation is made up of a thick layer of old constructions (Rossignol 2013) which likely [SOURCE: Page_225]

reduces root growth and access of deeper water sources. Thus, this possibility should only [SOURCE: Page_225]

goes for arboretum trees. [SOURCE: Page_225]

222 [SOURCE: Page_225]

Chapitre 5. Sources potentielles d’eau utilisées par les tilleuls argentés en ville [SOURCE: Page_226]

### 5.3.6. Conclusions

Our study provides important highlights concerning the ecohydrological functioning [SOURCE: Page_226]

of silver lindens in urban areas. As already observed, it seems that the atmospheric urban heat [SOURCE: Page_226]

island could strongly impact urban soil through temperature increase and moisture decrease, [SOURCE: Page_226]

both factors promoting soil evaporation during warmer months. For largest urban trees, the [SOURCE: Page_226]

decrease of soil water content makes urban soil more susceptible to evaporation during the [SOURCE: Page_226]

year. Our results also indicate that street trees rely on soil water in the 10-20 cm as a more or [SOURCE: Page_226]

less evaporated mixture between rainwater and non-potable water inputs, even if they are not [SOURCE: Page_226]

subjected to any irrigation strategy in Paris. However, this opportunity of non-potable water [SOURCE: Page_226]

as a source should be only punctual and doesn’t prevent street trees from water stress. [SOURCE: Page_226]

Additional soil water real-time measurements are needed to characterize the intensity of [SOURCE: Page_226]

evaporation and severity of water stress of the trees planted in streets in Paris. Nevertheless, [SOURCE: Page_226]

these results allow rethinking urban soil management and irrigation in order to preserve them [SOURCE: Page_226]

from excessive evaporation, especially under climate change. Reducing soil evaporation could [SOURCE: Page_226]

be through soil greening as in arboretum and park. However, as root growth and water-use [SOURCE: Page_226]

strategies are specific, on-going long-term researches are nevertheless crucial in order to [SOURCE: Page_226]

enhance our understanding about the ecohydrology of common urban trees, especially under [SOURCE: Page_226]

climate change. [SOURCE: Page_226]

Beatrice Rizzo, Christophe Simonetti and his team of foresters for authorizing us to work on [SOURCE: Page_226]

Paris streets and parks trees and for their priceless help during this study. We are very grateful [SOURCE: Page_226]

to Philippe Raynaud (MNHN, arboretum of Chèvreloup) for his precious help for arboretum [SOURCE: Page_227]

tree samples. At last, we would like to thank our students Tania de Almedia, Laure-Hélène [SOURCE: Page_227]

## DISCUSSION     GENERALE    ET PERSPECTIVES DE LA THESE

278 [SOURCE: Page_281]

279 [SOURCE: Page_282]

Chapitre 6. Discussion générale et perspectives de la thèse [SOURCE: Page_283]

Aujourd’hui, plus de la moitié de la population mondiale vit en ville. La présence d’arbres en milieu urbain, de par leur simple fonctionnement, participe à l’amélioration de la qualité de vie des citadins. Cependant, l’efficacité des services écologiques rendus par les arbres en ville dépend en grande partie de leur santé, de leur fonctionnement général et de leur survie, et donc des conditions abiotiques et biotiques dans lesquelles ils se trouvent. De nombreuses études ont mis en évidence l’importante mortalité et la faible espérance de vie des arbres en ville dues au stress hydrique, cette surmortalité touchant particulièrement les jeunes arbres nouvellement plantés. Le présent projet de thèse a ainsi cherché à répondre prioritairement aux questions suivantes : [SOURCE: Page_283]

(1) Comment la croissance et la mise en réserves des tilleuls argentés sont-elles influencées par leur situation en alignement à Paris ? [SOURCE: Page_283]

(2) Existe-t-il une mise en place de stratégies particulières pour ces arbres ? [SOURCE: Page_283]

(3) Ces stratégies changent-elles avec l’âge de l’arbre ? [SOURCE: Page_283]

(4) Quelles sont les sources d'eau potentielles utilisées par les arbres en ville ? [SOURCE: Page_283]

Les prochains paragraphes s’attacheront à répondre à ces questions au regard des résultats obtenus au cours de la thèse. Une seconde partie permettra de mettre en lumière les limites liées à cette étude, tout en proposant des éléments de perspectives afin de permettre une meilleure compréhension du fonctionnement hydrique et carboné des arbres plantés en rue. Enfin, nous reviendrons plus concrètement sur la qualité potentielle des services écosystémiques rendus par les arbres plantés en rue à la lumière de ces nouvelles connaissances. [SOURCE: Page_283]

280 [SOURCE: Page_283]

Chapitre 6. Discussion générale et perspectives de la thèse [SOURCE: Page_284]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Summary: Technical section focusing on 6.1. résultats généraux et principales réponses aux hypothèses de la thèse
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
